Maximum Weight Independent Set in Hereditary Classes of Ordered Graphs
Paweł Rafał Bieliński, Marta Piecyk, Paweł Rzążewski

Abstract
The complexity of classical computational problems in graph classes defined by forbidding induced subgraphs is one of the central topics of algorithmic graph theory. Recently, there has been a growing interest in the complexity of such problems in ordered graphs , i.e., graphs with a fixed linear ordering of vertices. Such an approach allows us to investigate the boundary of tractability more closely. However, most results so far concern coloring problems. In this paper, we focus on the complexity of the Maximum Weight Independent Set ( MWIS ) problem in classes of ordered graphs. For every ordered graph H H , we classify the complexity of MWIS in ordered graphs that exclude H H as an induced subgraph into one of the following cases: 1. solvable in polynomial time, 2. solvable in quasipolynomial time, 3. solvable in subexponential time, 4. NP -hard. Notably, case (3) contains only one well-structured family of H H obtained from two nested edges by adding isolated vertices in a specific way. Thus, our results yield an almost complete complexity dichotomy for MWIS in classes of ordered graphs defined by a single forbidden induced subgraph into cases solvable in quasipolynomial time and those that are NP -hard.



1
Introduction

†
†

PRB and PRz were supported by the National Science Centre grant number 2024/54/E/ST6/00094.

In the
Max Independent Set
(
MIS
) problem, we are given a graph
G
and we ask for a largest
independent set
in
G
, i.e., a largest set of pairwise nonadjacent vertices. The
Max Weight Independent Set
(
MWIS
) problem is a generalization of
MIS
, where we are given a graph
G
together with a weight function
\mathfrak{w}:V(G)\to\mathbb{Q}_{>0}
, and we ask for an independent set of maximum total weight. Both problems are among the most fundamental and well-studied problems in algorithmic graph theory.
They are also known to be notoriously hard:
MIS
is among Karp’s 21
NP
-complete problems
[
33
]
, cannot be solved in subexponential time under the Exponential-Time Hypothesis (ETH)
[
32
]
, is a canonical
W[1]
-hard problem
[
16
]
, and is hard to approximate
[
29
]
, even in parameterized setting
[
11
,
35
]
.

A typical approach when dealing with such a hard problem is to consider restricted instances in order to understand the boundary of tractability.
Usually, one considers instances coming from certain
hereditary
graph classes, i.e., classes of graphs closed under vertex deletion.
Every such class can be characterized by a family of forbidden induced subgraphs.
Thus, as a starting point, it is natural to ask for which graphs
H
the
MWIS
problem can be solved efficiently on
H
-free graphs
, i.e., graphs that do not contain
H
as an induced subgraph.

The complexity study of
MWIS
in
H
-free graphs is one of the central topics in algorithmic graph theory.
It turns out that the crucial role is played by the family of subcubic forests whose every component is a path or a
subdivided claw
, i.e., a tree with at most one vertex of degree 3.
Let us denote by
\mathcal{S}
the family of such forests.

It was already observed by Alekseev
[
3
]
that if
H\notin\mathcal{S}
, then
MIS
(and thus,
MWIS
) is
NP
-hard in
H
-free graphs.
Indeed, this observation follows from combining two known results. First,
MIS
is
NP
-hard for subcubic graphs
[
21
]
, and they in particular exclude any graph
H
with a vertex of degree at least 4.
The second ingredient is the so-called ‘Poljak’s subdivision trick’
[
43
]
:
if we subdivide one edge of a graph
G
twice, i.e., we replace it by a four-vertex path, then the size of a largest independent set increases by exactly one.
Thus, if
H
has a cycle or two vertices of degree 3 in a single component, then subdividing each edge of any instance of
MIS
sufficiently many times yields an equivalent instance that is
H
-free.

It turns out that polynomial-time algorithms for
MWIS
are only known for small graphs
H\in\mathcal{S}
,
most notably, if
H
is a path on at most 6 vertices
[
27
]
, a graph obtained from a three-leaf star by subdividing one edge once
[
37
]
, or a graph whose every component is a three-leaf star
[
9
]
.
On the other hand, we know no
NP
-hardness results for
MWIS
in
H
-free graphs with
H\in\mathcal{S}
and the general belief is that the problem is polynomial-time solvable in all such cases.
This belief is supported by the fact that for every
H\in\mathcal{S}
, the
MWIS
problem can be solved in quasipolynomial time when restricted to
H
-free graphs
[
24
,
42
,
22
]
.
Note that this is a strong indication that the problem is not
NP
-hard in any of these cases,
as otherwise every problem in
NP
would be solvable in quasipolynomial time, which is considered unlikely.

In this paper we follow the line of research described above, but we focus on hereditary classes of
ordered
graphs, i.e., graphs with a fixed linear order of their vertex set.
We aim to understand for which ordered graphs
H
the
MWIS
problem can be solved efficiently in
H
-free ordered graphs (where an ordered induced subgraph is an induced subgraph that preserves the relative order of its vertices).
We emphasize that the ordered setting can be seen as a refinement of the unordered one.
Indeed, forbidding a single
unordered
graph is equivalent to forbidding
all
of its possible orderings as ordered induced subgraphs.
Instead, we can forbid only some of the orderings, which allows us to trace the boundary of tractability more precisely.

Let us remark that the idea of looking at classical graph-theoretic problems in an ordered setting is not new.
For example, there is a rich (and still growing) line of research concerning Turán-type
[
45
,
34
,
8
,
38
]
and Ramsey-type
[
6
,
15
,
19
]
problems in ordered graphs. There are also some results on the chromatic number
[
4
,
10
]
or structural properties
[
39
,
44
]
of certain classes of ordered graphs.
However, the algorithmic results seem to be much scarcer: we are only aware of recent work on the complexity of (
List
)
k
-
Coloring
in classes defined by excluding one induced ordered subgraph
[
28
,
41
]
.
In particular, to the best of our knowledge, the complexity of
MWIS
in this setting was not studied before.
In this paper, we fill this gap by proving the following theorem.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
H
be an ordered graph with at least two vertices.
The
MWIS
problem in ordered
H
-free graphs can be solved in:

1.

polynomial time, if
H
is an induced subgraph of a graph in

-free and, in particular,

Interestingly,

-free graphs remain one of few open cases concerning the complexity of
List
3-
Coloring

[
28
,
41
]
, which is another indication that graphs from this class might be difficult to understand. Their structure does not seem restricted enough to allow for (simple) efficient algorithms, nor rich enough to allow for (simple) hardness results.

Another indication that

-free graphs have complicated structure is the fact that unordered graphs that admit an ordering that avoids

as a subgraph are precisely graphs of
queue number
1
[
30
]
. Such graphs are
NP
-hard to recognize
[
31
]
and thus, there is little hope for their simple structural characterization.
On the other hand, unordered graphs that admit an ordering that avoids

as a subgraph are known as graphs of
stack number
1
[
7
]
and they are precisely outerplanar graphs – a rather simple class
[
12
]
.
These two examples already illustrate that graphs defined by forbidding even a very simple ordered substructure might be quite complicated. Furthermore, the ordering of the vertices in a forbidden subgraph can have a huge impact on the structure of the graphs in the corresponding hereditary class.

Now, let us proceed to a high-level overview of the proof of
Theorem

1.1
.

Technical overview.

It is not hard to observe (see
Lemma

2.1
) that if
H^{\prime}
is obtained from
H
by adding some isolated vertices at the beginning or at the end of the ordering, then the
MWIS
problem in
H^{\prime}
-free graphs can be reduced to solving the same problem in the more restrictive class of
H
-free graphs.

. Indeed,
MWIS
is polynomial-time solvable in

-free graphs, but
NP
-hard in

-free graphs.

Thus, from now on let us focus on the case that
H
has no isolated vertices at the beginning or at the end of the ordering. In what follows, the input graph is always denoted by
G
and its number of vertices is denoted by
n
.

The cases listed in
Theorem

1.1
(1) are actually quite simple.
One can readily verify that ordered

-free graphs are co-comparability graphs and ordered

-free and ordered

-free graphs are chordal – in both cases
MWIS
can be solved in polynomial time by classical algorithms
[
25
,
26
]
.
Interestingly, the above inclusions are in fact equivalences in the sense that every co-comparability graph admits an ordering that avoids

and every chordal graph admits an ordering that avoids

(and the reverse ordering avoids

).

1.1
(1), i.e.,

,
but the algorithm for

}{{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}{{}}}{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}
}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{}{}{}{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 16.24 0.85 C 17.58 3.18 21.79 3.18 23.13 0.85}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
is much simpler and has better running time.

Note that

-free graphs contain all bipartite graphs: indeed, if we take any bipartite graph and order its vertices so that all vertices from one part come before all vertices from the other part, we get an

-free ordered graph.

MWIS
in bipartite graphs can be solved in polynomial time by a reduction to the maximum matching problem
[
36
]
. Therefore, when working with

-free graphs, we should be aware that we are in particular solving the (unordered) bipartite case and thus, we should not hope for a simple dynamic programming or branching algorithm.

In fact, our approach is to start with an

-free instance of
MWIS
and reduce it, in quasipolynomial time, to a quasipolynomial number of bipartite instances, each of which can be solved in polynomial time.
A
seagull
is a triple of vertices
x\prec y\prec z
such that
y
is adjacent to both
x
and
z
.
We observe that graphs that do not contain any seagull are bipartite.
Thus, we employ a branching scheme in order to destroy all seagulls.

First, we observe that every two seagulls in an

-free graph share a vertex or are joined by an edge.
Thus, if
x,y,z
is a seagull, then the closed neighborhood of one of
x,y,z
intersects at least one third of all seagulls in the graph.
We branch on such a vertex
v
, i.e., we guess whether it is in the solution or not.
In the former case we remove
v
and all its neighbors from the graph (which destroys a constant fraction of all seagulls) and in the latter one we remove just
v
(which destroys at least one seagull – the one formed by
x,y,z
).
As the number of all seagulls is
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
, this branching scheme produces at most
n^{\mathcal{O}(\log n)}
instances, each of which has no seagulls and thus, can be solved in polynomial time.
Thus, the overall running time of the algorithm is
n^{\mathcal{O}(\log n)}
.

}{{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}{{}}}{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 24.11 0.85 C 25.45 3.18 29.66 3.18 31.01 0.85}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
for any fixed
k
.
Here we also use a branching scheme, but it is more involved than in the previous case.
The algorithm maintains a more structured instance, where the vertex set is partitioned into three consecutive parts
X,Y,Z
, such that
X
and
Z
are independent, while
Y
is the “workspace” that still requires handling. Initially, we take
X=Z=\emptyset
and
Y=V(G)
.
If
Y=\emptyset
, then the graph is bipartite and the problem can be solved in polynomial time, so the goal is to shrink
Y
until it becomes empty.

To this end, we split
Y
into two halves,
Y_{L}
and
Y_{R}
, and exhaustively guess the first
k
vertices of the optimum solution that lie in
Y_{R}
.
If
X\cup Y_{L}
or
Y_{R}\cup Z
, say the former one, is independent, then we can move
Y_{L}
to
X
, thereby reducing the size of the workspace by at least half.
Thus, we may assume that both
X\cup Y_{L}
and
Y_{R}\cup Z
contain an edge; call such edges
internal
.
Now the forbidden pattern

(k)

yields a key structural property: the closed neighborhood of every internal edge on one side of the split intersects every internal edge on the opposite side.
Consequently, if both sides still contain an internal edge, then the neighborhood of one endpoint of any edge on the sparser side (i.e., one with fewer internal edges) intersects a constant fraction of the internal edges on the denser side.

This leads to an internal branching procedure, analogous in spirit to the

-case: we guess whether such a vertex belongs to the solution or not.
If we exclude it, we destroy at least one internal edge; if we include it, we delete its closed neighborhood and thus, destroy a constant fraction of all internal edges.
We continue until one side of the split becomes independent; the number of instances produced this way is
n^{\mathcal{O}(\log n)}
.
At that point, we absorb the independent side into
X
or
Z
, thereby obtaining a new structured instance whose workspace has size at most half of the previous one.

Repeating this halving procedure recursively yields quasipolynomially many subinstances, each eventually reduced to a bipartite graph.
Thus, the recursion has two levels: the outer one halves the workspace, while the inner one destroys a constant fraction of internal edges. Altogether, this gives running time
n^{\mathcal{O}(\log^{2}n)}
.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
– we just remark that the general case is slightly more complicated.
Here the general strategy is similar in spirit to the case of

(k)

, but the relevant structure of the considered instances is different (and the arguments are more involved).
We start by partitioning the vertex set into three consecutive blocks
A,B,C
of roughly equal size and view them as a
chain
, that is, a sequence of consecutive sets (called
links
) in which edges may appear only inside one set, between two consecutive sets, or between the first and the last one.
The algorithm repeatedly
refines
such a chain by splitting one selected link into two consecutive sublinks.

The main subroutine is a refinement lemma:
for any chosen link, in quasipolynomial time we can branch into quasipolynomially many instances in such a way that this link is replaced by two smaller consecutive links.
Again the crucial structural observation is that, since

is forbidden, there is always a vertex whose closed neighborhood intersects a constant fraction of the “unwanted” edges.
This again shows that in quasipolynomial time we can produce
n^{\mathcal{O}(\log n)}
branches, in each of which the chosen link can indeed be split into two parts.

Starting from the initial chain
(A,B,C)
, we repeatedly refine the smallest link of
type
A
(i.e., one that originates from
A
), the smallest link of
type
B
, and the smallest link of type
C
.
In each refinement step the size of a smallest link of the given type is halved, so after
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
refinements for each type, we arrive at a chain containing an empty link of each of the three types.
These three empty links separate the graph into three pairwise nonadjacent subinstances, each missing one of sets
A,B,C
and therefore having size at most roughly
2n/3
.
We solve these subinstances recursively and sum their optimum values.

Summarizing the above, the algorithm has three nested recursive layers.
One application of the refinement lemma costs
n^{\mathcal{O}(\log n)}
branches.
Repeating refinements until an empty link of each type appears creates a tree of depth
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
and hence the number of instances produced is
n^{\mathcal{O}(\log^{2}n)}
.
Finally, each leaf instance splits into three pairwise nonadjacent subinstances of size at most roughly
2n/3
each, which contributes the third logarithmic factor in the exponent.
Thus, the overall running time is
n^{\mathcal{O}(\log^{3}n)}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.33 0.92 C 3.4 9.33 20.23 9.33 23.29 0.92}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

}{{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}{{}}}{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}
}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{}{}{}{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.57 0.69 C 10.36 2.48 13.27 2.48 15.06 0.69}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
.
The first step is a standard branching on high-degree vertices
[
5
]
.
Setting a threshold
\tau=n^{1/3}
, we branch on every vertex of degree at least
\tau
until all remaining instances have maximum degree below
\tau
.
This already costs only subexponential time, so from now on we may assume that every vertex has degree at most
\tau
.

The next step is to partition the ordered vertex set into consecutive segments
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{t}
so that each segment
Z_{i}
comes with an edge
x_{i}y_{i}
spanning it, and edges between different segments may appear only between consecutive ones.
Such a partition can be obtained by greedily picking, at each step, an edge leaving the current prefix whose right endpoint is as far to the right as possible.
Thus, after this partition, the graph behaves like a path of segments.
The forbidden pattern

implies that once the neighborhood of
\{x_{i},y_{i}\}
is removed, the rest of the segment
Z_{i}
becomes independent.

Now we look for a place where the instance can be split.
If some small segment (i.e., of size at most
n^{2/3}
) appears “in the middle” of the graph, then by branching on its intersection with the optimum solution we separate the graph into two independent subinstances, each of size at most roughly
2n/3
, and recurse on both sides.
Otherwise, every segment meeting the middle part is large, so there can be only few of them.
In this case we branch on a set of size
\mathcal{O}(n^{2/3})
containing the two “boundary” small segments together with the neighborhoods of all
x_{i},y_{i}
for the middle segments
Z_{i}
.
After removing this set, the middle part becomes bipartite, while the remaining left and right parts are again multiplicatively smaller and can be solved recursively.

Thus, every branch either is solved directly or reduces the problem to instances of size at most about
2n/3
, while paying only a subexponential branching cost per level.
The overall running time is
n^{\mathcal{O}(n^{2/3}\log^{3}n)}
.

Finally, let us say a few words about the hardness counterpart of
Theorem

1.1
.
We show several hardness reductions, each of which is tailored to a specific family of forbidden ordered graphs
H
.
Typically, we start with an unordered instance of
MIS
, then we use the Poljak’s subdivision trick to subdivide certain edges (possibly many times), and then we order the resulting graph in a way that ensures that
H
is not an induced subgraph.
It is possibly worth mentioning that in vast majority of cases we can actually exclude certain graphs
H
as a
subgraph
, which is a stronger condition than excluding them as an induced subgraph.

Organization of the paper.

In
Section

2
we give present definitions and preliminary results.
In
Section

3
we present the algorithms for the positive cases of
Theorem

1.1
.
In particular, statement (1) is proved in
Section

3.1
;
statement (2) is proved in
Section

);
and statement (3) is proved in
Section

3.5
.
In
Section

4
we prove the hardness results for the negative cases of
Theorem

1.1
.
Finally,
Section

5
concludes with open problems and directions for future research.

2
Preliminaries

All logarithms in the paper are binary.
Throughout the paper, we consider vertex-weighted graphs.
We always assume that all computations on weights can be performed in constant time.
For a vertex-weighted graph
(G,\mathfrak{w})
and a set
X\subseteq V(G)
, we define
\mathfrak{w}(X)=\sum_{v\in X}\mathfrak{w}(v)
.

Ordered graphs.

Let
G
be a graph with a fixed linear ordering of its vertex set.
We write
v\prec u
if
v
is before
u
in the ordering, and
v\preceq u
if
v\prec u
or
v=u
.
For a set of vertices
X
, we write
X\prec v
if
x\prec v
for every
x\in X
, and
v\prec X
if
v\prec x
for every
x\in X
. We also write, e.g.,
x\prec Y
for
\{x\}\prec Y
.
We define operators
\succ
and
\succeq
analogously.

The ordering of vertices induces the lexicographic ordering of edges: for distinct edges
uv,u^{\prime}v^{\prime}
, where
u\prec v
and
u^{\prime}\prec v^{\prime}
, we write that
uv\prec u^{\prime}v^{\prime}
if either
u\prec u^{\prime}
or
u=u^{\prime}
and
v\prec v^{\prime}
.

By
N(v)
we denote the neighborhood of
v
in
G
, and by
N[v]
we denote the closed neighborhood of
v
in
G
, i.e.,
N[v]=N(v)\cup\{v\}
.
We say that
u
is a
left neighbor
(resp.,
right neighbor
) of
v
if
u\in N(v)
and
u\prec v
(resp.,
v\prec u
).

The
mirror image
of an ordered graph
G
is the ordered graph obtained from
G
by reversing the order of its vertices.
By the
underlying graph
of an ordered graph
G
, we mean the unordered graph obtained from
G
by forgetting the order of its vertices.

A
segment
is a set of consecutive vertices in the ordering. A
prefix
(resp.,
suffix
) is a segment that contains the first (resp., last) vertex in the ordering.
An edge
uv
is a
under
and edge
u^{\prime}v^{\prime}
if
u^{\prime}\leq u\prec v\prec v^{\prime}
.
An edge
uv
is
maximal
if there is no other edge
u^{\prime}v^{\prime}
such that
u^{\prime}\preceq u\preceq v\preceq v^{\prime}
.
For two edges
cross
whenever they induce

.

MWIS
in ordered graphs.

By
\alpha(G)
we denote the independence number of a graph
G
, i.e., the maximum size of an independent set in
G
.
For a vertex-weighted graph
(G,\mathfrak{w})
, by
\alpha(G,\mathfrak{w})
we denote the maximum weight of an independent set in
G
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
H
be an ordered graph, and let
H^{*}
be the graph obtained from
H
by adding an isolated vertex before all vertices of
H
, and an isolated vertex after all vertices of
H
.
Then,
MWIS
in
H
-free graphs and
MWIS
in
H^{*}
-free graphs are polynomially equivalent.

Proof.

Note that every graph that is
H
-free is also
H^{*}
-free, so a reduction from
MWIS
in
H
-free graphs to
MWIS
is
H^{*}
-free graphs is trivial.
Thus, let us show a Turing reduction from
MWIS
in
H^{*}
-free graphs to
MWIS
in
H
-free graphs.

Let
(G,\mathfrak{w})
be an instance of
MWIS
, where
G
is
H^{*}
-free and has
n
vertices.
We exhaustively guess the first and the last vertex of some fixed optimum solution
I^{*}
; call these vertices, respectively,
x
and
y
.
Clearly it only makes sense to consider choices when
xy\notin E(G)
. We note that if
|I^{*}|=1
, it might happen that
x=y
.

This step results in at most
n^{2}
branches; consider one of them.
We remove all vertices preceding
x
, all vertices following
y
, and the closed neighborhood of
\{x,y\}
.
Let
G^{\prime}
be the resulting graph. Note that is it
H
-free as any induced copy of
H
, together with
x
and
y
,
gives an induced copy of
H^{*}
in
G
.

Now we observe that in the branch where
x,y
were guessed correctly,

I^{*}\setminus\{x,y\}
is an optimum solution for
MWIS
in
(G^{\prime},\mathfrak{w})
.
Thus, we reduced solving
MWIS
on
(G,\mathfrak{w})
to solving
MWIS
on
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
instances, each of which is
H
-free and has at most
n
vertices.
∎

Repeated application of
Lemma

2.1
yields the following.

Corollary 2.2
.

Let
H
be an ordered graph and and let
k\geq 1
be a fixed integer.
Let
H^{*}
be the graph obtained from
H
by adding
k
isolated vertices before all vertices of
H
, and another
k
isolated vertices after all vertices of
H
.
Then,
MWIS
in
H
-free graphs and
MWIS
in
H^{*}
-free graphs are polynomially equivalent.

3
Algorithms

In this section we present the algorithms for the positive cases of
Theorem

1.1
.
By
Corollary

2.2
it suffices to present algorithms for the cases when
H
is exactly

,

,

(k)

(for statement (1) of
Theorem

1.1
),

(k)

,

(k)
(k)
(k)

(for statement (2)), and

(k)
(k)

(for statement (3)).

3.1
Polynomial-time cases: excluding

,

, or

(k)

Let us start with proving
Theorem

1.1
(1).

Theorem 3.1
.

MWIS
in can be solved in polynomial time in

-free graphs.

Proof.

Let us observe that every

-free graph is a comparability graph.
Indeed, if
G
is an ordered

-free graph, then (the reflexive closure of) the relation of being a right neighbor
is a partial order.
Now, the claim follows by recalling that
MWIS
can be solved in polynomial time in comparability graphs
[
26
]
.
∎

Theorem 3.2
.

MWIS
in can be solved in polynomial time in

-free and in

-free graphs.

Proof.

As

-free graphs are exactly the mirror images of

-free graphs, it suffices to prove the claim for

-free graphs.
Let us observe that every

-free graph is chordal:
the ordering of
V(G)
is a perfect elimination ordering (see also
[
28
]
).
As
MWIS
is polynomial-time solvable in chordal graphs
[
25
]
, the claim follows.
∎

Theorem 3.3
.

For every fixed
k
,
MWIS
can be solved in polynomial time in

(k)

-free graphs.

Proof.

Let
G
be a

(k)

-free ordered graph with consecutive vertices
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}
.
Let
\mathfrak{w}
be the weight function on
V(G)
.

The algorithm is based on dynamic programming, processing the subsequent prefixes of the vertex set.
We introduce a dynamic programming table
\mathsf{Tab}[i,I]
, where
i\in\{0,\ldots,n\}
and
I\subseteq\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{i}\}
is an independent set of size at most
k
.

We say that an independent set
I^{*}
is
compatible
with
(i,I)
if
I^{*}\subseteq\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{i}\}
and

Case A:
|I|=k
:

the last
k
vertices of
I^{*}
are
I
,

Case B:
|I|<k
:

I^{*}=I
.

The intended value in
\mathsf{Tab}[i,I]
is the maximum weight of an independent set compatible with
(i,I)
.
Clearly, having computed all entries of
\mathsf{Tab}[i,I]
, we can return
\max_{I}\mathsf{Tab}[n,I]
.

We initialize
\mathsf{Tab}[0,\emptyset]=0
and all other values to
-\infty
.
Suppose now that
i\geq 1
and we have computed all entries
\mathsf{Tab}[i-1,\cdot]
.
We aim to compute
\mathsf{Tab}[i,I]
for some fixed independent set
I
contained in
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{i}\}
.

If
|I|<k
, we simply store the weight of
I
.
Now suppose that
|I|=k
.
If
v_{i}\notin I
, we set
\mathsf{Tab}[i,I]=\mathsf{Tab}[i-1,I]
.
So consider the case
v_{i}\in I
.
For an independent set
I^{\prime}\subseteq\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{i-1}\}
, we write
I^{\prime}\sqsubset I
if either
I^{\prime}=I\setminus\{v_{i}\}
or
I^{\prime}
is obtained from
I
by removing
v_{i}
and adding a vertex
u
such that
u\prec I
and
uv_{i}\notin E
.
We set

\mathsf{Tab}[i,I]=\max_{I^{\prime}\sqsubset I}\mathsf{Tab}[i-1,I^{\prime}]+\mathfrak{w}(v_{i}).

(1)

It is straightforward to verify that the value of
\mathsf{Tab}[i,I]
is at least its intended value.
Let us argue that it is not larger, i.e., the value set in step (
1
) corresponds to the weight of some independent set compatible with
(i,I)
.
Let
I^{\prime}\sqsubset I
such that
\mathsf{Tab}[i,I]=\mathsf{Tab}[i-1,I^{\prime}]+\mathfrak{w}(v_{i})
.
If
|I^{\prime}|<k
, then the claim is clear.
So suppose that
|I^{\prime}|=k
, i.e.,
I^{\prime}=I\setminus\{v_{i}\}\cup\{u\}
for some
u\prec I
.

Let
I^{*}
be a maximum-weight independent set in
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{i-1}\}
compatible with
I^{\prime}
.
Clearly,
\mathsf{Tab}[i,I]=\mathfrak{w}(I^{*}\cup\{v_{i}\})
. We claim that
I^{*}\cup\{v_{i}\}
is independent.
As each of
I^{*},I
is independent, suppose that there is
w\in I^{*}
which is adjacent to
v_{i}
.
As
v_{i}u\notin E
and
I^{*}
is compatible with
I^{\prime}
, we must have
w\prec u
.
However, now the set of vertices
\{w\}\cup I^{\prime}\cup\{v_{i}\}
induces a copy of

(k)

in
G
, a contradiction.

The number of all entries in
\mathsf{Tab}[\cdot,\cdot]
is bounded by
n^{k+1}
, i.e., is polynomial in
n
.
As each entry is computed in polynomial time, the overall running time is also polynomial.
∎

{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.49 0.85 C 1.83 3.18 6.04 3.18 7.38 0.85}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}

{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 16.24 0.85 C 17.58 3.18 21.79 3.18 23.13 0.85}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
.
This algorithm is significantly simpler than the one for general
k
and has a lower running time, so it serves as a good warm-up for the more general case.

Theorem 3.4
.

MWIS
in
n
-vertex

-free graphs can be solved in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(\log n)}
.

Let us start with some observations.

Let
G
be an ordered graph.
A
seagull
is a triple
(x,y,z)
of distinct vertices of
G
such that
x\prec y\prec z

and
xy,yz\in E(G)
. Thus,
G[\{x,y,z\}]
is either a triangle or

.

Lemma 3.5
.

An ordered graph with no seagulls is bipartite.

Proof.

Suppose that
G
is an ordered graph with no seagulls.
We can assume that
G
has no isolated vertices, as their existence does not influence bipartiteness.
Thus, we can partition the vertices of
G
into the following sets:

\displaystyle X=\{v~|~N(v)\prec v\}\quad\text{and}\quad Y=\{v~|~N(v)\succ v\}.

We observe that each of
X
and
Y
is an independent set.
Thus,
G
is indeed bipartite.
∎

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
G
be a

-free ordered graph.
Then,
G
has no two seagulls that are disjoint and nonadjacent.

Proof.

For contradiction, suppose that
G
has two disjoint and nonadjacent seagulls
(x,y,z)
and
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime})
.
If
y^{\prime}\prec y
, then
\{x^{\prime},y^{\prime},y,z\}
is an induced copy of

in
G
.
If
y^{\prime}\succ y
, then
\{x,y,y^{\prime},z^{\prime}\}
is an induced copy of

in
G
.
In both cases we reach a contradiction, which completes the proof.
∎

Combining
Lemma

3.5
and
Lemma

3.6
, we obtain the following.

Corollary 3.7
.

Let
G
be a

-free ordered graph that is not bipartite.
Then there is
v\in V(G)
that belongs to a seagull and such that
N[v]
intersects one third of all seagulls.
Furthermore, such
v
can be found in polynomial time.

Proof.

Since
G
is not bipartite, by
Lemma

3.5
it contains a seagull
(x,y,z)
.
By
Lemma

3.6
we know that
N[\{x,y,z\}]
intersects all seagulls in
G
.
Thus, by averaging, there is
v\in\{x,y,z\}
such that
N[v]
intersects one third of all seagulls.
The running time bound follows from the fact that the number of all seagulls is
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
,
so exhaustive enumeration works in polynomial time.
∎

Now we are ready to present our algorithm.

Proof of
Theorem

3.4
.

Let
(G,\mathfrak{w})
be an instance of
MWIS
, where
G
has
n
vertices and is

-free.

Our algorithm is a recursive procedure.
The base case is that the instance graph is bipartite.
Then, we solve the problem in polynomial time using the Kőnig-Egerváry theorem and flow techniques
[
36
]
. Note that this in particular covers the case that
n\leq 2
.

Thus, assume that
G
is not bipartite.
Let
v
be a vertex given by applying
Corollary

3.7
on
G
.
We branch on
v
, i.e., we call the algorithm recursively for the instance
G-v
and for the instance
G-N[v]
.
In the first branch we seek for independent sets that do not contain
v
, and in the second branch we seek for independent sets that contain
v
.
We return the maximum of the value found in the first branch, and the value found in the second branch plus
\mathfrak{w}(v)
.

The correctness of the algorithm is obvious. Let us argue about the running time.
We will measure the potential of the instance by the number
\mu
of seagulls.
If
\mu=0
, the graph is bipartite and thus the instance can be solved in polynomial time.
The number of instances created by branching is given by the recursive inequality

F(\mu)\leq F(\mu-1)+F(2\mu/3),

which solves to
\mu^{\mathcal{O}(\log\mu)}
(see, e.g.,
[
18
]
for similar recursions and how to solve them).
Recalling that
\mu\leq n^{3}
, we conclude that overall running time of the algorithm is bounded by
n^{\mathcal{O}(\log n)}
.
∎

3.3
Excluding

(k)

We are ready to show a quasipolynomial-time algorithm for the case

for any fixed
k
.

Theorem 3.8
.

For every fixed
k
,
MWIS
in
n
-vertex

(k)

-free graphs can be solved in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(\log^{2}n)}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, let us assume that
k\geq 1
.
Let
(G,\mathfrak{w})
be an instance of
MWIS
such that
G=(V,E)
is an
n
-vertex

(k)

-free ordered graph.
Both
G
and
\mathfrak{w}
remain unchanged throughout the course of the algorithm, and we will represent certain induced subgraphs of
G
by their vertex sets.

Structured instances.

Throughout the algorithm, we will work with instances of
MWIS
equipped with some additional structure.
A
structured instance
of
MWIS
is a triple
(X,Y,Z)
, where:

•

X,Y,Z
are pairwise disjoint subsets of
V
,

•

X\prec Y\prec Z
, and

•

X,Z
are independent sets.

For a structured instance
\mathcal{I}=(X,Y,Z)
, we define
\alpha(\mathcal{I}):=\alpha(G[X\cup Y\cup Z],\mathfrak{w})
.

The set
Y
of a structured instance
(X,Y,Z)
is called the
workspace
. We will measure the size of a structured instance by the size of its workspace.
We can see the initial instance
(G,\mathfrak{w})
of
MWIS
as the structured instance
\mathcal{I}_{0}:=(\emptyset,V,\emptyset)
with measure
n
.
On the other hand, a structured instance of measure 0 is bipartite, thus it can be solved in polynomial time.

For structured instances
\mathcal{I}=(X,Y,Z)
and
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}=(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},Z^{\prime})
,
we say that
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is a
subinstance
of
\mathcal{I}
if
X\cap(X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime}\cup Z^{\prime})\subseteq X^{\prime}
and
Z\cap(X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime}\cup Z^{\prime})\subseteq Z^{\prime}
.
In other words,
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
was obtained from
\mathcal{I}
by deleting some vertices and moving some vertices from the workspace to the independent sets. Note that the measure of
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
does not exceed the measure of
\mathcal{I}
.

The main idea of the algorithm is to reduce solving our original structured instance
(\emptyset,V,\emptyset)
to solving a number of structured subinstances, each of measure 0. We will do this by branching on carefully chosen vertices.

Main branching.

The following claim encapsulates the main step of the algorithm.

Claim 3.8.1
.

Given a structured instance
\mathcal{I}=(X,Y,Z)
of measure
\mu>1
,
in time
\mu^{\mathcal{O}(\log\mu)}
we can construct a family
\mathscr{I}
of
\mu^{\mathcal{O}(\log\mu)}
pairs
(\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})
such that:

1.

for every
(\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})\in\mathscr{I}
,
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is a structured subinstance of
\mathcal{I}
and
\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
,

2.

for every
(\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})\in\mathscr{I}
, the measure of
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is at most
\lceil\mu/2\rceil
, and

3.

\alpha(\mathcal{I})=\max_{(\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})\in\mathscr{I}}(\alpha(\mathcal{I}^{\prime})+\ell^{\prime})
.

Before we prove
3.8.1
, let us first argue that it is sufficient to prove the theorem.
We construct the recursion tree whose nodes are labeled by pairs
(\mathcal{I},\ell)
, where
\mathcal{I}
is a subinstance of
\mathcal{I}_{0}
and
\ell\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
.
The root of this tree is labeled by
(\mathcal{I}_{0},0)
.
Consider a node
d
that has not been processed yet; let it be labeled with the pair
(\mathcal{I},\ell)
.
If
\mathcal{I}
has a measure of at most 1, we do not do anything;
d
becomes a leaf of the recursion tree.
Otherwise, we apply
3.8.1
to
\mathcal{I}
, obtaining the family
\mathscr{I}
.
For each
(\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})\in\mathscr{I}
, we create a new child node of
d
and label it with
(\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})
.
We perform this construction exhaustively.

Note that all leaves of the recursion tree correspond to instances of measure at most 1 and they can be solved in polynomial time.
Indeed, if the measure is 0, the instance is bipartite.
If the measure is 1, we can guess whether the unique vertex of the workspace belongs to the sought-for optimum solution and continue with an instance of measure 0.
By processing the tree from leaves to the root and using
3
from
3.8.1
at each step, we can then solve
\mathcal{I}_{0}
(equivalently,
(G,\mathfrak{w})
).

Now let us argue about the running time.
The number of nodes in the recursion tree is proportional to the number of leaves,
which is given by the following inequality:

F(\mu)\leq\mu^{\mathcal{O}(\log\mu)}\ F(\lceil\mu/2\rceil)=\mu^{\mathcal{O}(\log^{2}\mu)}.

As local computation in each node (of measure
\mu
) requires time
\mu^{\mathcal{O}(\log\mu)}
, and the measure of
\mathcal{I}_{0}
is
n
,
we obtain that the overall running time is bounded by
n^{\mathcal{O}(\log^{2}n)}
, as claimed.

Proof of
3.8.1
.

Thus, we are left with proving
3.8.1
.
It will be convenient to define a refined variant of a structured instance.
A
refined instance
is a quadruple
(X,Y_{L},Y_{R},Z)
, where
Y_{L},Y_{R}
are disjoint sets such that
Y_{L}\prec Y_{R}
and
(X,Y_{L}\cup Y_{R},Z)
is a structured instance.
In other words, a refined instance is obtained from a structured instance
(X,Y,Z)
by splitting
Y
into a prefix
Y_{L}
and a suffix
Y_{R}
. Having this in mind, we say that
(X,Y_{L},Y_{R},Z)
is a
split
of
(X,Y,Z)
.
We also define
\alpha((X,Y_{L},Y_{R},Z))=\alpha((X,Y,Z))
.
We say that a refined instance
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}_{L},Y^{\prime}_{R},Z^{\prime})
is a subinstance of a refined instance
(X,Y_{L},Y_{R},Z)
if
X^{\prime}\subseteq X
,
Y^{\prime}_{L}\subseteq Y_{L}
,
Y^{\prime}_{R}\subseteq Y_{R}
, and
Z^{\prime}\subseteq Z
.

Let
\mathcal{I}=(X,Y,Z)
be a structured instance of measure
\mu>0
, i.e., with
Y\neq\emptyset
.

Creating a family
\mathscr{J}
of splits.

Let
Y=\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}\}
, where
y_{1}\prec y_{2}\prec\ldots\prec y_{m}
, and let
r=\lceil m/2\rceil
.
Define
Y_{L}=\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{r}\}
and
Y_{R}=\{y_{r+1},\ldots,y_{m}\}
, and note that
\mathcal{J}_{0}=(X,Y_{L},Y_{R},Z)
is a split of
\mathcal{I}
.
As an intermediate step towards the construction of
\mathscr{I}
, we will construct an auxiliary family
\mathscr{J}
.
Each element of
\mathscr{J}
is a pair
(\mathcal{J},\ell)
, where
\mathcal{J}
is a refined subinstance of
\mathcal{J}_{0}
, and
\ell\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
.

First, we exhaustively guess the first
k
vertices of the sought-after optimum solution
I^{*}
that are in
Y_{R}
, or the whole set
I^{*}\cap Y_{R}
if
|I^{*}\cap Y_{R}|<k
.
More formally, for every independent set
I\subseteq Y_{R}
of size at most
k
, we define a refined instance
\mathcal{J}^{I}=(X^{I},Y_{L}^{I},Y_{R}^{I},Z^{I})
which is a subinstance of
\mathcal{J}
, as follows:

•

X^{I}=X\setminus N(I)
,
Y_{L}^{I}=Y_{L}\setminus N(I)
,
Z^{I}=Z\setminus N(I)
,

•

if
|I|<k
, then
Y^{I}_{R}=\emptyset
,

•

if
|I|=k
and
y^{*}
is the last vertex of
I
, then
Y^{I}_{R}
is obtained from
Y_{R}
by removing
N[I]
and all vertices that precede
y^{*}
.

Furthermore, we set
\ell^{I}=\mathfrak{w}(I)
and include the pair
(\mathcal{J}^{I},\ell^{I})
into
\mathscr{J}
.
This concludes the construction of
\mathscr{J}
.
Clearly, the size of the constructed family is
\mathcal{O}(\mu^{k})
, i.e., polynomial in
\mu
as
k
is a constant.
Furthermore,
\mathscr{J}
can be constructed in polynomial time.

By the construction, it is clear that

\alpha(\mathcal{I})=\alpha(\mathcal{J}_{0})=\max_{(\mathcal{J},\ell)\in\mathscr{J}}\alpha(\mathcal{J})+\ell.

(2)

Now let us analyze the structure of instances in
\mathscr{J}
.
Fix any element
(\mathcal{J},\ell)\in\mathscr{J}
, where
\mathcal{J}=(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}_{L},Y^{\prime}_{R},Z^{\prime})
.
Since
\mathcal{J}
is a subinstance of
\mathcal{J}_{0}
and
k\geq 1
, we have that

|Y^{\prime}_{L}|\leq\lceil\mu/2\rceil\quad\text{ and }\quad|Y^{\prime}_{R}|\leq\lceil\mu/2\rceil.

(3)

Now, define
\mathsf{Left}=X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime}_{L}
and
\mathsf{Right}=Y^{\prime}_{R}\cup Z^{\prime}
, and
E_{L}=E(G[\mathsf{Left}])
and
E_{R}=E(G[\mathsf{Right}])
.
Let us consider the case that one of the sets
E_{L},E_{R}
is empty.
Suppose that
E_{L}=\emptyset
; the case
E_{R}=\emptyset
is analogous.
In such a case
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}=(X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime}_{L},Y^{\prime}_{R},Z^{\prime})
is a structured instance,
which is a subinstance of
\mathcal{I}
.
Furthermore, as
|Y^{\prime}_{R}|\leq\lceil|Y|/2\rceil
by (
3
),
we see that the measure of this structured instance is at most
\lceil|Y|/2\rceil=\lceil\mu/2\rceil
.
Thus, the pair
(\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})
can be inserted to the family
\mathscr{I}
that we construct.
We call
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
obtained from
\mathcal{J}
that way a
derived structure instance
.

Thus, the overall strategy is to branch on carefully selected vertices in order to make one of the sets
E_{L},E_{R}
empty.
This justifies measuring the size of a refined subinstance
\mathcal{J}
by the value
\xi(\mathcal{J})=|E_{L}|+|E_{R}|
.

The crucial structural observation is as follows.

Claim 3.8.2
.

Let
u,v\in\mathsf{Left}
(resp.,
\mathsf{Right}
) be such that
uv\in E
.
Then,
\mathsf{Right}\setminus N[\{u,v\}]
(resp.,
\mathsf{Left}\setminus N[\{u,v\}]
) is an independent set.

Proof of Claim 3.8.2:

Let
I
be the independent set for which the pair
(\mathcal{J},\ell)\in\mathscr{J}
was created.
Let
uv
be an edge with both endpoints in
\mathsf{Left}
(the other case is symmetric).
For contradiction, suppose that there are
u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Right}\setminus N[\{u,v\}]
such that
u^{\prime}v^{\prime}\in E
.
Since
Z^{\prime}\subseteq Z
is independent, at least one of
u^{\prime},v^{\prime}
must belong to
Y^{\prime}_{R}
.
In particular,
Y^{\prime}_{R}\neq\emptyset
, which means that
|I|=k
.

By the construction of
\mathcal{J}
, we have that
\mathsf{Left}\prec I\prec\mathsf{Right}
and
I
is nonadjacent to
\mathsf{Left}\cup\mathsf{Right}
.
Therefore, the set
\{u,v\}\cup I\cup\{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\}
induces a copy of

(k)

in
G
, a contradiction.

\lrcorner

Internal branching.

For each
(\mathcal{J},\ell)\in\mathscr{J}
, we perform branching described below.
Again, we can imagine this process as building a recursion tree
\mathsf{T}
, whose nodes are labeled with pairs
(\mathcal{J}^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})
,
where
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
is a subinstance of
\mathcal{J}
and
\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
.
The root of the tree is labeled by
(\mathcal{J},\ell)
.

Now, consider a node
d
of
\mathsf{T}
that has not been processed yet.
Let its label be
(\mathcal{J}^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})
, where
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}=(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}_{L},Y^{\prime}_{R},Z^{\prime})
.
We define
\mathsf{Left},\mathsf{Right},E_{L},E_{R}
for
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
as before.
If
E_{L}=\emptyset
or
E_{R}=\emptyset
, we do nothing and
d
becomes a leaf of the recursion tree.

So suppose otherwise, and by symmetry, assume that
|E_{L}|\geq|E_{R}|
. This means that
|E_{L}|\geq\xi(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})/2
.
Pick any edge
uv\in E_{R}
.
By
3.8.2
, we know that
N[\{u,v\}]
intersects all edges in
E_{L}
.
Consequently, there is
x\in\{u,v\}
such that
N[x]
intersects at least half of the edges in
E_{L}
, i.e., at least one quarter of the edges in
E_{L}\cup E_{R}
.

We add two children to
d
in
\mathsf{T}
.
One of them corresponds to
not including

x
in the solution and is labeled by the instance
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}_{L},Y^{\prime}_{R}\setminus\{x\},Z^{\prime}\setminus\{x\})
and number
\ell^{\prime}
.
The second child corresponds to
including

x
in the solution.
It is labeled by the instance
(X^{\prime}\setminus N[x],Y^{\prime}_{L}\setminus N[x],Y^{\prime}_{R}\setminus N[x],Z^{\prime}\setminus N[x])
, and the number
\ell^{\prime}+\mathfrak{w}(x)
.

Note that the measure of both created instances drops compared to the measure of
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
.
On one hand, removing just
x
decreases the measure by at least 1, as it destroys the edge
uv
.
On the other hand, removing
N[x]
from the graph decreases the measure to at most
|E_{L}|/2+|E_{R}|\leq 3\xi(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})/4
.

We proceed in this manner exhaustively.
Now, having built
\mathsf{T}
, consider a leaf node and let it be labeled with
(\mathcal{J}^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})
, where
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}=(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}_{L},Y^{\prime}_{R},Z^{\prime})
. As either
X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime}_{L}
or
Y^{\prime}_{R}\cup Z^{\prime}
is independent, there is a structured instance
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
derived from
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
. We include
(\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})
into
\mathscr{I}
.
This completes the definition of this family.

Correctness.

Let us verify that
\mathscr{I}
satisfies all properties listed in
3.8.1
.
The size of
\mathscr{I}
is equal to the number of leaves in all the recursion trees created for all members of
\mathscr{J}
.
The number of leaves of one such tree is given by a recursion

R(\xi)\leq R(\xi-1)+R(3\xi/4)=\xi^{\mathcal{O}(\log\xi)}.

Since
\xi=\mathcal{O}(\mu^{2})
, we obtain that the number of leaves on one recursion tree is
\mu^{\mathcal{O}(\log\mu)}
.
As the number of all trees is bounded by
|\mathscr{J}|=\mathcal{O}(\mu^{k})
, we conclude that the size of
\mathcal{I}
is
\mu^{\mathcal{O}(\log\mu)}
, as required. Furthermore, the family can clearly be enumerated in time
\mu^{\mathcal{O}(\log\mu)}
.

Property
1
from
3.8.1
follows directly from the construction.
Property
2
follows from (
3
) and the definition of a derived structured instance.
Finally,
3
follows easily from combining (
2
) with a standard bottom-up induction on a recursion tree. This completes the proof.
∎

3.4
Excluding

(k)
(k)
(k)

, but the details are significantly more involved.

Theorem 3.9
.

For every fixed
k
,
MWIS
in
n
-vertex

(k)
(k)
(k)

-free graphs can be solved in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(\log^{3}n)}
.

Chains.

Similarly to
Section

3.3
, we will work with instances equipped with some additional structure.
Let
(G,\mathfrak{w})
be an instance of
MWIS
, where
G=(V,E)
is an ordered

(k)
(k)
(k)

-free graph.
A
chain
(over
(G,\mathfrak{w})
) is a sequence
\mathcal{X}=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{r})
of
r\geq 3
pairwise disjoint subsets of
V
, called
links
,
such that

•

X_{1}\prec X_{2}\prec\ldots\prec X_{r}
,

•

if
uv
is an edge of
G
where
u\in X_{i}
and
v\in X_{j}
for
i<j
,
then either
j=i+1
or
i=1
and
j=r
.

We often identify chains with the unions of all links,
i.e., we write
G[\mathcal{X}]
for
G[\bigcup\mathcal{X}]
, and for a set
Y\subseteq V(G)
,
we write
\mathcal{X}-Y
for
(X_{1}\setminus Y,\ldots,X_{r}\setminus Y)
.
Furthermore, we define
\alpha(\mathcal{X})=\alpha(G[\mathcal{X}],\mathfrak{w})
.
Note that if
\mathcal{X}
is a chain, then every edge of
G[\mathcal{X}]
is either local (i.e., contained in one link),
or joins two cyclically consecutive links.

We say that a chain
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=(X^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,X^{\prime}_{r+1})
is a
refinement
of a chain
\mathcal{X}=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{r})

at

j\in[r]
if

•

for all
i<j
, we have
X^{\prime}_{i}\subseteq X_{i}
,

•

X^{\prime}_{j},X^{\prime}_{j+1}\subseteq X_{j}
, and

•

for all
i>j
, we have
X^{\prime}_{i+1}\subseteq X_{i}
.

In other words,
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
was obtained by splitting the link
X_{j}
into two sets and possibly removing some vertices.

We emphasize that we never require the links of a chain to be non-empty.
Actually, our approach is to start with a partition of
V
into three segments (note that this is a chain)
and keep refining this chain until we obtain some empty links, and thus disconnect the graph.

The following lemma encapsulates the main technical step of the algorithm:
that in quasipolynomial time, one can obtain a refinement of a given chain at any given link.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
k\geq 1
be an integer and let
(G,\mathfrak{w})
be an instance of
MWIS
such that
G
is an
n
-vertex

(k)
(k)
(k)

-free ordered graph.
Let
\mathcal{X}=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{r})
be a chain over
(G,\mathfrak{w})
and let
j\in[r]
.
In time
n^{\mathcal{O}(\log n)}
, we can construct a family
\mathscr{Z}
of
n^{\mathcal{O}(\log n)}
pairs
(\mathcal{Z},\ell)
, where
\mathcal{Z}
is a refinement of
\mathcal{X}
at
j
and
\ell\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
, such that

\alpha(\mathcal{X})=\max_{(\mathcal{Z},\ell)\in\mathscr{Z}}\alpha(\mathcal{Z})+\ell.

In the proof of
Lemma

3.10
we distinguish two cases: (1)
1<j<r
, (2)
j\in\{1,r\}
.
The general outline of the argument in both cases is the same, but the details differ.
Thus, we present these constructions separately.

Proof of
Lemma

3.10
for
1<j<r
.

The proof has two main steps.

Construction of
\mathscr{Y}
.

Let us first construct an auxiliary family
\mathscr{Y}
,
which corresponds to guessing the first
k
and the last
k
vertices of the solution in
X_{j}
.
More precisely, for each independent set
I\subseteq X_{j}
of size at most
2k
, we construct a chain
\mathcal{X}^{I}
as follows.
Let
I_{1}
be the set of the first
k
vertices of
I
, or
I_{1}=I
if
|I|<k
.
Let
I_{2}=I\setminus I_{1}
.
Let
v_{1}^{*}
be the last vertex of
I_{1}
(if it exists), and let
v_{2}^{*}
be the first vertex of
I_{2}
(if it exists).
Then,
\mathcal{X}^{I}
is obtained from
\mathcal{X}
by

1.

removing the vertices of
N[I]
, and

2.

if
|I|=2k
, then removing
\{x\in X_{j}\ |\ x\prec v_{1}^{*}\lor x\succ v_{2}^{*}\}
,
and otherwise removing all vertices of
X_{j}
.

That completes the construction of
\mathcal{X}^{I}
.
We emphasize that the links of
\mathcal{X}^{I}
are in one-to-one correspondence with the links of
\mathcal{X}
.

We add to
\mathscr{Y}
the pair
(\mathcal{X}^{I},\mathfrak{w}(I))
.
Note that
|\mathscr{Y}|=\mathcal{O}(n^{2k})
, i.e., is polynomial in
n
.
Furthermore, as each pair in
\mathscr{Y}
corresponds to exhaustively guessing (at most)
k
first and (at most)
k
last vertices of the solution that are in
X_{j}
, it clearly holds that

\alpha(\mathcal{X})=\max_{(\mathcal{Y},\ell)\in\mathscr{Y}}\alpha(\mathcal{Y})+\ell.

The following claim shows the crucial structural property of elements in
\mathscr{Y}
.

a
b
c
d
k
X^{I}_{j-1}
X^{I}_{j}
X^{I}_{j+1}
I_{1}
I_{2}

Figure 1:
Sets
X^{I}_{j-1}
,
X^{I}_{j}
,
X^{I}_{j+1}
, and independent sets
I_{1}
,

(each of size
k
) in
3.10.1
.
The orange subgraph cannot be induced, as otherwise, together with
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
, it creates the forbidden structure.

Claim 3.10.1
.

Let
(\mathcal{X}^{I},\ell^{I})
, where
\mathcal{X}^{I}=(X^{I}_{1},\ldots,X^{I}_{r})
, be a pair in
\mathscr{Y}
constructed for an independent set
I
.
There is no induced copy of

(k)

with edges
ac,bd
such that
a\in X^{I}_{j-1}
and
b,c\in X^{I}_{j}
, and
d\in X^{I}_{j+1}
.

Proof of Claim 3.10.1:

For contradiction, suppose that such a copy of

(k)

with distinguished vertices
a,b,c,d
exists.
Let
I^{\prime}
, where
b\prec I^{\prime}\prec c
, be the
k
-element independent set in this copy.

As
b,c\in X^{I}_{j}
, we have
|I|=2k
.
Let
I_{1}
(resp.,
I_{2}
) be the first (resp., last)
k
vertices of
I
.
Then we have
a\prec I_{1}\prec b\prec I^{\prime}\prec c\prec I_{2}\prec d
and there are no edges between
I_{1}\cup I_{2}
and
\{a,b,c,d\}\cup I^{\prime}
.
Consequently,
\{a,b,c,d\}\cup I_{1}\cup I^{\prime}\cup I_{2}
induces a copy of

(k)
(k)
(k)

in
G
(see
Fig.

1
), a contradiction.

\lrcorner

Construction of
\mathscr{Z}_{\mathcal{Y}}
.

Fix some
(\mathcal{Y},\ell)\in\mathscr{Y}
.
Now in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(\log n)}
we will construct a family
\mathscr{Z}_{\mathcal{Y}}
of pairs
(\mathcal{Z},\ell^{\prime})
, where

\mathcal{Z}
is a refinement of
\mathcal{Y}
(and thus of
\mathcal{X}
) at
j
and
\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
, such that

\alpha(\mathcal{Y})=\max_{(\mathcal{Z},\ell^{\prime})\in\mathscr{Z}_{\mathcal{Y}}}\alpha(\mathcal{Z})+\ell^{\prime}.

Let us point out that proving this would conclude the proof of the lemma (in the considered case).
Indeed, the family

\mathscr{Z}:=\bigcup_{(\mathcal{Y},\ell)\in\mathscr{Y}}\bigcup_{(\mathcal{Z},\ell^{\prime})\in\mathscr{Z}_{\mathcal{Y}}}(\mathcal{Z},\ell+\ell^{\prime})

satisfies all properties listed in the lemma, and the overall running time and the size of
\mathscr{Z}
are both bounded by
n^{\mathcal{O}(\log n)}
.

Thus, let us show how to construct
\mathscr{Z}_{\mathcal{Y}}
for
(\mathcal{Y},\ell)\in\mathscr{Y}
.
The process can be again described as a recursion tree
\mathsf{T}
.
We start with the root node which is labeled with the pair
(\mathcal{Y},0)
.
Next, we proceed exhaustively as follows.
Let
d
be a node of
\mathsf{T}
that was not processed yet,
and let it be labeled with
(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})
, where
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}=(Y^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,Y^{\prime}_{r})
.
If there are no edges from
Y^{\prime}_{j-1}
to
Y^{\prime}_{j}
or from
Y^{\prime}_{j}
to
Y^{\prime}_{j+1}
, then we stop the recursion,
i.e.,
d
will be a leaf of
\mathsf{T}
.

Otherwise, let
u^{*}
be the first vertex of
Y^{\prime}_{j}
that is adjacent to some vertex in
Y^{\prime}_{j+1}
,
and let
v^{*}
be the last vertex of
Y^{\prime}_{j}
that is adjacent to some vertex in
Y^{\prime}_{j-1}
.
If
v^{*}\prec u^{*}
, then we again stop the recursion and
d
becomes a leaf.

So now assume that
u^{*}\preceq v^{*}
, we emphasize that it is possible that
u^{*}=v^{*}
.
Let
S
be the set of vertices
y\in Y^{\prime}_{j}
such that
u^{*}\preceq y\preceq v^{*}
, and let
E_{1}
(resp.,
E_{2}
) be the set of edges with one endpoint in
Y^{\prime}_{j-1}
(resp.,
Y^{\prime}_{j+1}
), and the other in
S
.
We also define
\widetilde{S}\subseteq S
to be the set of the vertices adjacent to at least one edge of
E_{1}\cup E_{2}
(see
Fig.

2
).

u^{*}
v^{*}

Y^{\prime}_{j}

.

Claim 3.10.2
.

There exists a vertex
w
which is an endpoint of some edge in
E_{1}\cup E_{2}
and such that the set
N[w]
intersects at least
\frac{1}{2k+4}
-fraction of the edges of
E_{1}\cup E_{2}
.

Proof of Claim 3.10.2:

By symmetry, assume that
|E_{1}|\geq|E_{2}|
.
Let
U
be an independent set defined as follows.
We initialize
U=\emptyset
.
As long as
|U|<k
, we add (if exists) to
U
the first vertex of
\widetilde{S}\setminus N[U\cup\{u^{*}\}]
.
Note that if at the end of the procedure
|U|<k
,
then the set
N[U\cup\{u^{*}\}]
contains all vertices of
\widetilde{S}
, and thus intersects all the edges of
E_{1}
.

So now suppose that
|U|=k
.
Let us fix a neighbor
u^{\prime}
of
u^{*}
in
Y^{\prime}_{j+1}
– by the choice of
u^{*}
such a vertex exists.
Observe that the set
N[U\cup\{u^{*},u^{\prime}\}]
intersects all edges of
E_{1}
.
Indeed, suppose otherwise, i.e., there is an edge
xy\in E_{1}
with
x\in Y^{\prime}_{j-1}
,
y\in Y^{\prime}_{j}
and such that
x,y
are nonadjacent to
U\cup\{u^{*},u^{\prime}\}
.
Note that
x\prec u^{*}\prec U\prec y\prec u^{\prime}
,
and thus the set
U\cup\{u^{*},u^{\prime},x,y\}
induces in
G[\mathcal{Y}]
a copy of

(k)

with
x\in Y_{j-1}
,
u^{*},y\in Y_{j}
,
and
u^{\prime}\in Y_{j+1}
(see
Fig.

2
), which contradicts
3.10.1
.

Therefore, in any case, the set
N[U\cup\{u^{*},u^{\prime}\}]
intersects all the edges of
E_{1}
,
and thus there is a vertex
w\in U\cup\{u^{*},u^{\prime}\}

such that
N[w]
intersects at least
\frac{1}{k+2}
-fraction of edges in
E_{1}
.
Since
|E_{1}|\geq|E_{2}|
, we obtain that the set
N[w]
intersects at least
\frac{1}{2k+4}
-fraction of edges in
E_{1}\cup E_{2}
. Moreover, every vertex in
U\cup\{u^{*},u^{\prime}\}
is an endpoint of some edge in
E_{1}\cup E_{2}
, which completes the proof of the claim.

\lrcorner

Let us remark that the vertex
w
from
3.10.2
can be found in polynomial time.
Now we are ready to define child nodes for
d
; the nodes will correspond to taking
w
to the solution or not, i.e., we add two child nodes of
d
that are labeled respectively with:

(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}-N[w],\ell^{\prime}+\mathfrak{w}(w))\quad\text{and}\quad(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}-\{w\},\ell^{\prime}).

Clearly, the introduced pairs satisfy the following:

\alpha(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime})=\max\bigl(\alpha(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}-N[w])+\mathfrak{w}(w),\alpha(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}-\{w\})\bigr).

This concludes the construction of
\mathsf{T}
.

Now let us bound the number of leaves.
Let
\mu=|E_{1}|+|E_{2}|
.
Let us point out that the vertices
u^{*}
and
v^{*}
might change, but always the sets
S,E_{1},E_{2}
are subsets of the corresponding sets for the parent node.
By
3.10.2
, in the branch corresponding to taking
w
to the solution, the number of edges in
E_{1}\cup E_{2}
decreases at least by
\frac{\mu}{2k+4}
, and in the branch corresponding to not taking
w
to the solution, we remove
w
which is an endpoint of at least one edge of
E_{1}\cup E_{2}
.
Therefore, the bound on the number of leaves in
\mathsf{T}
is given by the recursive inequality:

F(\mu)\leq F\left(\frac{2k+3}{2k+4}\;\mu\right)+F(\mu-1),

which solves to
\mu^{\mathcal{O}(\log\mu)}
(recall that
k
is a constant).
Applying
\mu\leq n^{2}
, the number of leaves in
\mathsf{T}
is bounded by
(n^{2})^{\mathcal{O}(\log n^{2})}=n^{\mathcal{O}(\log n)}
.

Now we are ready to define the family
\mathscr{Z}_{\mathcal{Y}}
.
Consider a leaf node of
\mathsf{T}
, let it be labeled with
(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})
, and let
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}=(Y^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,Y^{\prime}_{r})
.
Then one of the following holds:

1.

there are no edges between
Y^{\prime}_{j-1}
and
Y^{\prime}_{j}
,

2.

there are no edges between
Y^{\prime}_{j}
and
Y^{\prime}_{j+1}
,

3.

for the first vertex
u^{*}\in Y^{\prime}_{j}
that is adjacent to some vertex in
Y^{\prime}_{j+1}
,
and for the last vertex
v^{*}\in Y^{\prime}_{j}
that is adjacent to some vertex in
Y^{\prime}_{j-1}
,
it holds that
v^{*}\prec u^{*}
.

In each of these cases, we add to
\mathscr{Z}_{\mathcal{Y}}
the pair
(\mathcal{Z},\ell^{\prime})
, where
\mathcal{Z}
is defined as follows, depending on the case:

1.

\mathcal{Z}=(Y^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,Y^{\prime}_{j-1},\emptyset,Y^{\prime}_{j},Y^{\prime}_{j+1},\ldots,Y^{\prime}_{r})
,

2.

\mathcal{Z}=(Y^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,Y^{\prime}_{j-1},Y^{\prime}_{j},\emptyset,Y^{\prime}_{j+1},\ldots,Y^{\prime}_{r})
,

3.

\mathcal{Z}=(Y^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,Y^{\prime}_{j-1},Y^{\prime}_{j,\leftarrow},Y^{\prime}_{j,\rightarrow},Y^{\prime}_{j+1},\ldots,Y^{\prime}_{r})
,
where
Y^{\prime}_{j,\leftarrow}=\{x\in Y^{\prime}_{j}\ |\ x\preceq v^{*}\}
and
Y^{\prime}_{j,\rightarrow}=Y^{\prime}_{j}\setminus Y^{\prime}_{j,\leftarrow}
.

We repeat this for every leaf node of
\mathsf{T}
.
It is straightforward to verify that
\mathscr{Z}_{\mathcal{Y}}
satisfies all required properties.

This completes the proof of the lemma in the case if
1<j<r
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

3.10
for
j\in\{1,r\}
.

Let us assume that
j=1
, as the case
j=r
is symmetric.
The proof follows the same outline as the one for the previous case.

Construction of
\mathscr{Y}
.

First, let us construct an auxiliary family
\mathscr{Y}
which this time corresponds to guessing the last (at most)
k
vertices in
X_{1}
and the first (at most)
k
vertices in

that belong to the solution.

Formally, for every pair of sets

,

,
such that

as follows.

.

,

if

, then removing

, and otherwise removing all vertices from

,

if

.

We add the pair

to
\mathscr{Y}
.
Similarly as in the previous case, we have

, and

\alpha(\mathcal{X})=\max_{(\mathcal{X}^{I},\ell^{I})\in\mathscr{Y}}\alpha(\mathcal{X}^{I})+\ell^{I}.

Now, we have the following analogue of

.

Claim 3.10.3
.

.

\mathscr{Z}_{\mathcal{Y}}
.

Fix some
(\mathcal{Y},\ell)\in\mathscr{Y}

of pairs

, where
\mathcal{Z}
is a refinement of

(and thus of

) at

and
\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
,
such that

\alpha(\mathcal{Y})=\max_{(\mathcal{Z},\ell^{\prime})\in\mathscr{Z}_{\mathcal{Y}}}\alpha(\mathcal{Z})+\ell^{\prime}.

Recall that this will complete the proof, as the family

\mathscr{Z}:=\bigcup_{(\mathcal{Y},\ell)\in\mathscr{Y}}\bigcup_{(\mathcal{Z},\ell^{\prime})\in\mathscr{Z}_{\mathcal{Y}}}(\mathcal{Z},\ell+\ell^{\prime})

satisfies all properties listed in the lemma.

Thus, let us show how to construct
\mathscr{Z}_{\mathcal{Y}}
for
(\mathcal{Y},\ell)\in\mathscr{Y}
.
Again, we will build a recursion tree
\mathsf{T}
whose root it labeled by

.
We proceed exhaustively as follows.
Let

be a node of

that was not processed yet,
and let it be labeled with

, where
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}=(Y^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,Y^{\prime}_{r})
.

If there are no edges from

to

or from

to

, then we stop the recursion,

will be a leaf of

.
Otherwise, let

be the first vertex of

that is adjacent to some vertex in

,
and let

be the last vertex of

, then we again stop the recursion and

.
