Claim 3.10.4
.

There exists a vertex
w
which is an endpoint of some edge in
E_{1}\cup E_{2}
and such that the set
N[w]
intersects at least
\frac{1}{2k+4}
-fraction of the edges in
E_{1}\cup E_{2}
.

Proof of Claim 3.10.4:

Suppose first that
|E_{1}|\geq|E_{2}|
.
Let us define an independent set
U
as follows. We start with
U=\emptyset
.
As long as
|U|<k
, we add (if exists) to
U
the last vertex of
\widetilde{S}\setminus N[U\cup\{v^{*}\}]
.
Note that if at the end of the procedure
|U|<k
, then
N[U\cup\{v^{*}\}]
contains all vertices of
\widetilde{S}
.
So assume now that
|U|=k
, and let
v^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}_{r}
be the neighbor of
v^{*}
– it exists by the choice of
v^{*}
.
We observe that the set
N[U\cup\{v^{*},v^{\prime}\}]
intersects all the edges of
E_{1}
.
Indeed, suppose otherwise, and let
xy
be an edge such that
x\in Y^{\prime}_{1}
,
y\in Y^{\prime}_{2}
and
x,y
are nonadjacent to
U\cup\{v^{*},v^{\prime}\}
.
Note that we have
x\prec U\prec v^{*}\prec y\prec v^{\prime}
.
Then the set
U\cup\{v^{*},v^{\prime},x,y\}
induces in
G[\mathcal{Y}]
a copy of

(k)

with
x,v^{*}\in Y_{1}
,
y\in Y_{2}
, and
v^{\prime}\in Y_{r}
, which contradicts
3.10.3
.
Therefore, in both cases, the set
N[U\cup\{v^{*},v^{\prime}\}]
intersects all the edges of
E_{1}
,
and by the pigeonhole principle, there is
w\in U\cup\{v^{*},v^{\prime}\}
such that
N[w]
intersects at least
\frac{1}{k+2}
-fraction of the edges in
E_{1}
.
Since
|E_{1}|\geq|E_{2}|
, the set
N[w]
intersects at least
\frac{1}{2k+4}
-fraction of the edges in
E_{1}\cup E_{2}
, which completes the proof of the claim in this case.

So now suppose that
|E_{1}|<|E_{2}|
.
Again, we define an independent set
U
as follows.
As long as
|U|<k
, we add (if exists) to
U
the first vertex of
\widetilde{S}\setminus N[U\cup\{u^{*}\}]
.
If at the end of the procedure
|U|<k
, then
N[U\cup\{u^{*}\}]
contains all vertices of
\widetilde{S}
, and thus, intersects all edges in
E_{2}
.
So we can assume that
|U|=k
.
Similarly as before, using
3.10.3
, we observe that the set
N[U\cup\{u^{*},u^{\prime}\}]
, where
u^{\prime}
is a neighbor of
u^{*}
in
Y^{\prime}_{2}
, intersects all edges in
E_{2}
.

Thus, again using the pigeonhole principle and the fact that
|E_{2}|>|E_{1}|
,
we conclude that some vertex in
U\cup\{u^{*},u^{\prime}\}
satisfies the statement of the claim.

\lrcorner

Let us go back to the definition of the children of a node
d
of the recursion tree
\mathsf{T}
.
For a vertex
w
given by
3.10.4
, we create child nodes labeled, respectively, by

(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}-N[w],\ell^{\prime}+\mathfrak{w}(w))\quad\text{and}\quad(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}-\{w\},\ell^{\prime}).

Similarly as in the previous case we have

\alpha(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime})=\max\bigl(\alpha(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}-N[w])+\mathfrak{w}(w),\alpha(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}-\{w\})\bigr).

This concludes the construction of
\mathsf{T}
.
Again, we can bound the number of leaves
\mathsf{T}
of this tree by
\mu^{\mathcal{O}(\log\mu)}
which is bounded by
n^{\mathcal{O}(\log n)}
as
\mu\leq n^{2}
.

Finally, we are ready to define
\mathscr{Z}_{\mathcal{Y}}
in this case.
Consider a leaf node of
\mathsf{T}
, let it be labeled with
(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})
, and let
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}=(Y^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,Y^{\prime}_{r})
.
Then one of the following holds:

1.

there are no edges between
Y^{\prime}_{1}
and
Y^{\prime}_{r}
,

2.

there are no edges between
Y^{\prime}_{1}
and
Y^{\prime}_{2}
,

3.

for the first vertex
u^{*}\in Y^{\prime}_{1}
that is adjacent to some vertex in
Y^{\prime}_{2}
, and for the last vertex
v^{*}\in Y^{\prime}_{1}
that is adjacent to some vertex in
Y^{\prime}_{r}
, it holds that
v^{*}\prec u^{*}
.

In each of these cases, we add to
\mathscr{Z}_{\mathcal{Y}}
the pair
(\mathcal{Z},\ell^{\prime})
, where
\mathcal{Z}
is defined as follows, depending on the case:

1.

\mathcal{Z}=(\emptyset,Y^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,Y^{\prime}_{r})
,

2.

\mathcal{Z}=(Y^{\prime}_{1},\emptyset,Y^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,Y^{\prime}_{r})
,

3.

\mathcal{Z}=(Y^{\prime}_{1,\leftarrow},Y^{\prime}_{1,\rightarrow},Y^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,Y^{\prime}_{r})
,
where
Y^{\prime}_{1,\leftarrow}=\{x\in Y^{\prime}_{1}\ |\ x\preceq v^{*}\}
and
Y^{\prime}_{1,\rightarrow}=Y^{\prime}_{1}\setminus Y^{\prime}_{1,\leftarrow}
.

We repeat this for every leaf node of
\mathsf{T}
.
Again, it is straightforward to verify that
\mathscr{Z}_{\mathcal{Y}}
satisfies all required properties.
This completes the proof.
∎

Equipped with
Lemma

3.10
, we are ready to prove
Theorem

3.9
.

Proof of
Theorem

3.9
.

Without loss of generality assume that
k\geq 1
.
Let
(G,\mathfrak{w})
be an
n
-vertex instance of
MWIS
such that
G=(V,E)
is a

(k)
(k)
(k)

-free ordered graph.
The algorithm is recursive, with the trivial base case that
n\leq 2
.
So assume that
n\geq 3
and let
V=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\}
where
v_{1}\prec v_{2}\prec\ldots\prec v_{n}
.
Define:

\displaystyle A=

\displaystyle\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{\lceil\frac{n}{3}\rceil}\},

\displaystyle B=

\displaystyle\{v_{\lceil\frac{n}{3}\rceil+1},\ldots,v_{\lceil\frac{2n}{3}\rceil}\},

\displaystyle C=

\displaystyle\{v_{\lceil\frac{2n}{3}\rceil+1},\ldots,v_{n}\}.

Note that
\mathcal{Q}=(A,B,C)
is a chain over
(G,\mathfrak{w})
.
Furthermore, each its link is of size at most
\lceil\frac{n}{3}\rceil
.

We will keep refining
\mathcal{Q}
, obtaining new chains.
Note that each link of such a chain originates either from
A
or from
B
or from
C
.
We will call such links, respectively, of type
A
,
B
, or
C
.

We recursively construct a labeled rooted tree
\mathsf{T}
as follows.
We start with a root labeled with
(\mathcal{Q},0)
.
Now, consider a node
d
of
\mathsf{T}
that was not processed yet.
Let
d
be labeled with
(\mathcal{Q}^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})
, where
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}=(Q^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,Q^{\prime}_{r})
is a chain and
\ell\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
.

For
\lambda\in\{A,B,C\}
, let
i_{\lambda}
be an index of a smallest link of type
\lambda
,
where ties are resolved arbitrarily.
If
|Q^{\prime}_{i_{A}}|=|Q^{\prime}_{i_{B}}|=|Q^{\prime}_{i_{C}}|=0
, we stop the recursion and
d
becomes a leaf of
\mathsf{T}
.
So suppose there is some
\lambda\in\{A,B,C\}
such that
Q^{\prime}_{i_{\lambda}}\neq\emptyset
.
We call
Lemma

3.10
for
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
and
j=i_{\lambda}
,
obtaining the family
\mathscr{Z}
.
For each member
(\mathcal{Z},\ell)
of this family, we create a child node for
d
and label it with
(\mathcal{Z},\ell)
.
This concludes the construction of
\mathsf{T}
.

Let us bound the number of leaves in the tree.
First note that each node of
\mathsf{T}
has at most
n^{\mathcal{O}(\log n)}
children.
Furthermore, note that if
d^{\prime}
is a child node of
d
, obtained by refining the chain at
i_{\lambda}
for some
\lambda\in\{A,B,C\}
, then the smallest link of type
\lambda
in the instance corresponding to
d^{\prime}

has size at most half of the size of the smallest link of type
\lambda
in the instance corresponding to
d
.
This means that on each root-to-leaf path in
\mathsf{T}
, the same value of
\lambda
is chosen at most
\log n
times, and thus, the depth of
\mathsf{T}
is at most
3\log n
.
Consequently, the number of leaves of
\mathsf{T}
is at most
n^{\mathcal{O}(\log^{2}n)}
.

Finally, by
Lemma

3.10
,
we can compute
\alpha(G,\mathfrak{w})=\alpha(\mathcal{Q})
by solving the instances corresponding to leaves of
\mathsf{T}
.

So let us focus on analyzing the structure of such an instance
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}=(Q^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,Q^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}})
.
Recall that since we are at a leaf of
\mathsf{T}
,
for each
\lambda\in\{A,B,C\}
there is
i_{\lambda}
such that
Q^{\prime}_{i_{\lambda}}
is of type
\lambda
and
Q^{\prime}_{i_{\lambda}}=\emptyset
. Clearly
i_{A}<i_{B}<i_{C}
.
Let us partition
\bigcup\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
into three sets (see
Fig.

5
):

\displaystyle V_{1}=

\displaystyle\ Q^{\prime}_{1}\cup\ldots\cup Q^{\prime}_{i_{A}-1}\cup Q^{\prime}_{i_{C}+1}\ldots\cup Q^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}

\displaystyle V_{2}=

\displaystyle\ Q^{\prime}_{i_{A}+1}\cup\ldots\cup Q^{\prime}_{i_{B}-1}

\displaystyle V_{3}=

\displaystyle\ Q^{\prime}_{i_{B}+1}\cup\ldots\cup Q^{\prime}_{i_{C}-1}.

Note that since
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
is a chain, these three sets are pairwise nonadjacent.
Thus, we obtain that

\alpha(\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})=\alpha(G[V_{1}],\mathfrak{w})+\alpha(G[V_{2}],\mathfrak{w})+\alpha(G[V_{3}],\mathfrak{w}),

and each summand can be computed independently by calling the algorithm recursively the corresponding induced subgraph of
G
.
Furthermore, note that
V_{1}
is disjoint with
B
,
V_{2}
is disjoint with
C
, and
V_{3}
is disjoint with
A
.
Consequently, each of these sets is of size at most
2\lceil n/3\rceil
.
This means that the depth of the recursion is bounded by
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
.
Summing up, the overall running time is upper-bounded by
n^{\mathcal{O}(\log^{3}n)}
.
This completes the proof.
∎

Q^{\prime}_{1}
Q^{\prime}_{i_{A}}
Q^{\prime}_{i_{B}}
Q^{\prime}_{i_{C}}
Q^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}
A
B
C

Figure 5:
Sets
Q^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,Q^{\prime}_{r}
forming a chain
Q^{\prime}
in the proof of
Theorem

3.9
. Dotted blocks denote empty sets. Blocks in orange, blue, and green represent, respectively, the sets that belong to
V_{1}
,
V_{2}
, and
V_{3}
.

3.5
Excluding

(k)
(k)

In this section, we prove
Theorem

1.1
(3): for every fixed
k
, there is a
subexponential-time
algorithm for
MWIS
in

(k)
(k)

-free graphs.
More precisely, we show the following result.

Theorem 3.11
.

For every fixed
k
,
MWIS
in
n
-vertex

(k)
(k)

-free graphs can be solved in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(n^{2/3}\cdot\log^{3}n)}
.

Proof.

Let
k
be a fixed integer and let
(G,\mathfrak{w})
be an instance of
MWIS
, where
G
is

(k)
(k)

-free and has
n
vertices.
Note that we do not update the value of
n
throughout the run of the algorithm.
Let
\tau=n^{1/3}
.

Step 1. Branching on high-degree vertices.

First, we perform a standard branching on high-degree vertices.
If there is a vertex
v
of degree at least
\tau
, we branch on including or not including
v
in the solution.
The number of instances created in this phase is given by the recursion

F(n)\leq F(n-1)+F(n-\tau),

which is solved by
n^{\mathcal{O}(n/\tau)}=2^{\mathcal{O}(n^{2/3}\log n)}
.
Each instance
(G^{\prime},\mathfrak{w})
created in this phase has maximum degree at most
\tau
.
Furthermore, it is an induced subgraph of
G
, so in particular
G^{\prime}
is

(k)
(k)

-free.

Step 2. Partitioning
V(G^{\prime})
into segments.

Consider one instance
(G^{\prime},\mathfrak{w})
created in Step 1 and let
n^{\prime}=|V(G^{\prime})|
.
If
n^{\prime}=1
, the problem is obvious, so let us assume that
n^{\prime}\geq 2
.
We can also assume that
G^{\prime}
is connected; otherwise, we can call the algorithm for each component of
G^{\prime}
separately.

In the next Claim, we will define some sequence
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{t}
of subsets of
V(G^{\prime})
.
For
i\in[t]
, we introduce a notation
Z_{\leq i}:=\bigcup_{i^{\prime}\leq i}Z_{i^{\prime}}
.
We define
Z_{<i}
and
Z_{>i}
analogously.

Claim 3.11.1
.

There is a sequence
(x_{i},y_{i},Z_{i})_{i=1}^{t}
, for some
t\geq 1
, such that each
x_{i},y_{i}\in V(G^{\prime})
and
Z_{i}\subseteq V(G^{\prime})
,
that satisfies the following properties.

1.

for each
i\in[t]
, the set
Z_{\leq i}
is a prefix of
V(G^{\prime})
;

2.

for each
i\in[t]
, we have
x_{i}y_{i}\in E(G^{\prime})
,

3.

for each
i\in[t]
, it holds that
x_{i}\preceq Z_{i}\preceq y_{i}
and
y_{i}\in Z_{i}
,

4.

for each
i\in[t]
, the only vertices of
\bigcup_{i^{\prime}\leq i}Z_{i^{\prime}}
that might have edges to
V(G^{\prime})\setminus Z_{\leq i}
are in
Z_{i}
,

5.

sets
Z_{1},Z_{2},\ldots,Z_{t}
form a partition of
V(G^{\prime})
.

Proof of Claim 3.11.1:

We construct the sequence iteratively.
Let
x_{1}
be the first vertex of
G^{\prime}
and let
y_{1}
be the last neighbor of
x_{1}
;
it exists since
G^{\prime}
is connected and has at least two vertices.
Let
Z_{1}
denote the set of vertices
v
such that
x_{1}\preceq v\preceq y_{1}
.
Clearly, properties (P1)-(P4) are met.

Now, suppose we have defined
(x_{1},y_{1},Z_{1}),\ldots,(x_{i},y_{i},Z_{i})
for some
i\geq 1
.
If
y_{i}
is the last vertex of
G^{\prime}
, we finish the construction (i.e.,
t=i
);
note that property (P5) is met in this case.
So, suppose otherwise. We aim to define
(x_{i+1},y_{i+1},Z_{i+1})
.

Let
U=V(G^{\prime})\setminus Z_{\leq i}
. By property (P1),
U
consists of all vertices that are after
Z_{i}
.
Since
G^{\prime}
is connected, there must be an edge from
Z_{\leq i}
to
U
.
Let
x_{i+1}y_{i+1}\in E(G^{\prime})
be an edge, so that:

•

x_{i+1}\in Z_{\leq i}
and
y_{i+1}\in U
,

•

y_{i+1}
is furthest possible.

By (P4), we have
x_{i+1}\in Z_{i}
.
We define
Z_{i+1}
to be the set of
v
such that
Z_{i}\prec v\preceq y_{i+1}
.
Properties (P1), (P2), (P3) are clearly satisfied.
Property (P4) follows from the choice of
x_{i+1}y_{i+1}
.
This completes the
(i+1)
-th step of the construction.

\lrcorner

Let
(x_{i},y_{i},Z_{i})_{i=1}^{t}
be as in
3.11.1
.
Note that property (P4) implies that

(\star)

edges between sets
Z_{i},Z_{j}
, for
i<j
, might exist only if
j=i+1
.

We also denote
\mathsf{Roof}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{t}\{x_{i},y_{i}\}
.

Step 3. Picking independent sets inside
Z_{i}
.

For each
i\in[t]
, we proceed as follows.
Processing vertices from left to right, we greedily pick pairwise nonadjacent vertices in
Z_{i}\setminus N[\mathsf{Roof}]
.
We finish once we pick
k
vertices or when there are no more vertices in
Z_{i}\setminus N[\mathsf{Roof}]
that are nonadjacent to the selected ones.
Let
\mathsf{First}_{i}
denote the set of at most
k
vertices picked in this step.

Next, processing vertices from right to left, we greedily pick pairwise nonadjacent vertices in
Z_{i}\setminus(N[\mathsf{Roof}]\cup N[\mathsf{First}_{i}])
.
Again, we stop once we pick
k
vertices or once there are no more possible vertices to choose.
Let
\mathsf{Last}_{i}
denote the set of at most
k
vertices picked in this step.
Summing up,
\mathsf{First}_{i}\cup\mathsf{Last}_{i}
is an independent set of size at most
2k
, and there are no edges between this set and
\{x_{i},y_{i}\}
.

We also observe the following.

Claim 3.11.2
.

For each
i\in[t]
, the set
Z_{i}\setminus N[\{x_{i},y_{i}\}\cup\mathsf{First}_{i}\cup\mathsf{Last}_{i}]
is independent.

Proof of Claim 3.11.2:

For contradiction, suppose there an edge
uv
in
Z_{i}\setminus N[\{x_{i},y_{i}\}\cup\mathsf{First}_{i}\cup\mathsf{Last}_{i}]
.
Since
Z_{i}\setminus N[\{x_{i},y_{i}\}\cup\mathsf{First}_{i}\cup\mathsf{Last}_{i}]
is non-empty, we have
|\mathsf{First}_{i}|=|\mathsf{Last}_{i}|=k
.
Moreover, by the choice of
\mathsf{First}_{i}
and
\mathsf{Last}_{i}
, for every vertex
w\in Z_{i}\setminus N[\{x_{i},y_{i}\}\cup\mathsf{First}_{i}\cup\mathsf{Last}_{i}]
, it holds
\mathsf{First}_{i}\prec w\prec\mathsf{Last}_{i}
.
Consequently, vertices
\{x_{i},u,v,y_{i}\}\cup\mathsf{First}_{i}\cup\mathsf{Last}_{i}
induce a copy of

(k)
(k)

in
G^{\prime}
, a contradiction.

\lrcorner

Step 4. Some more branching to split the instance.

For
i\in[t]
, we say that
Z_{i}
is
large
if it has at least
n^{\prime}/\tau\leq n^{2/3}
vertices;
otherwise
Z_{i}
is
small
.
Let
\mathsf{Left}
(resp.,
\mathsf{Right}
) consist of the first (resp., last)
\lfloor n^{\prime}/3\rfloor
vertices of
G^{\prime}
,
and let
\mathsf{Middle}=V(G^{\prime})\setminus(\mathsf{Left}\cup\mathsf{Right})
.

We consider two cases.

Case 1: There is a small set intersecting
\mathsf{Middle}
.

Let
i\in[t]
be such that
Z_{i}
is small and
Z_{i}\cap\mathsf{Middle}\neq\emptyset
.
By
(\star)
, there are no edges between
Z_{<i}
and
Z_{>i}
.
Furthermore,
|Z_{<i}|\leq\frac{2}{3}n^{\prime}
and
|Z_{>i}|\leq\frac{2}{3}n^{\prime}
.

We exhaustively guess the intersection of a fixed optimum solution with
Z_{i}
;
in each branch we remove
Z_{i}
and all neighbors of vertices that are chosen to be in the solution.

This breaks the instance into two independent subinstances, one contained in
Z_{<i}
and another contained in
Z_{>i}
,
each of size at most
\frac{2}{3}n^{\prime}
. Thus, on each of these subinstances, we can call the algorithm recursively.

The running time bound in this case is given by the recursive inequality

T(n^{\prime})\leq 2^{\mathcal{O}(n^{\prime}/\tau)}T(2n^{\prime}/3),

which is solved by

T(n^{\prime})\leq 2^{\mathcal{O}(n^{\prime}\log n^{\prime}/\tau)}=2^{\mathcal{O}(n^{2/3}\log n)}.

Case 2: All small sets (if any) are contained in
\mathsf{Left}\cup\mathsf{Right}
.

Let
\ell
be the largest value of
i
such that
Z_{i}
is small and
Z_{i}\subseteq\mathsf{Left}
, if such a value exists.
Similarly, let
r
be the smallest value of
i
such that
Z_{i}
is small and
Z_{i}\subseteq\mathsf{Right}
, if such a value exists.
In what follows, let us assume that both
\ell
and
r
are as defined; the other cases are analogous (and slightly simpler).
We proceed similarly as in Case 1.

Note that removing
Z_{\ell}
and
Z_{r}
splits the graph into three parts:
Z_{<\ell}
,
\bigcup_{i=\ell+1}^{r-1}Z_{i}
, and
Z_{>r}
.
Again, there are no edges between distinct parts.

The first and the last part are multiplicatively smaller than the original instance – each has at most
n^{\prime}/3
vertices.
On the other hand, the middle part can be arbitrarily large. In fact, it might contain almost all vertices of
G^{\prime}
.
However, by the choice of
\ell
and
r
, we know that all sets
Z_{i}
that end up in the middle part are large.
Let
t^{\prime}=(r-1)-(\ell+1)+1
, i.e., the number of sets
Z_{i}
that belong to the middle part.
Since
n^{\prime}\geq\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{r-1}|Z_{i}|\geq t^{\prime}n^{\prime}/\tau
, we conclude that
t^{\prime}\leq\tau
.

Let
S=Z_{\ell}\cup Z_{r}\cup N_{G^{\prime}}\left[\bigcup_{i=\ell+1}^{r-1}\left(\{x_{i},y_{i}\}\cup\mathsf{First}_{i}\cup\mathsf{Last}_{i}\right)\right]
.
Note that
|S|\leq 2\cdot n/\tau+(2+2k)\tau\cdot\tau=\mathcal{O}(n^{2/3})
.
Again, we exhaustively guess the intersection of a fixed optimum solution with
S
.
This results in at most
2^{|S|}=2^{\mathcal{O}(n^{2/3})}
branches, in each of which we remove
S
and the neighbors of vertices chosen to be included in the solution.

Now, we obtain three instances that can be solved independently: two of them — one contained in
\mathsf{Left}
and the other contained in
\mathsf{Right}
— are small, i.e., have at most
2n^{\prime}/3
vertices.
Let
G^{\prime\prime}
be the middle instance.
By
3.11.2
and
(\star)
,
G^{\prime\prime}
consists of a number of independent sets,
where only possible edges between distinct sets might appear if sets are consecutive.
This means that
G^{\prime\prime}
is bipartite, and thus
MWIS
on
G^{\prime\prime}
can be solved in polynomial time.

Summing up, the running time bound in this case is given by recursive inequality

T(n^{\prime})\leq 2^{\mathcal{O}(n^{2/3})}\left(2\;T(2n^{\prime}/3)+n^{\prime\mathcal{O}(1)}\right)\leq 2^{\mathcal{O}(n^{2/3})}T(2/3\cdot n^{\prime}),

which is solved by

T(n^{\prime})\leq 2^{\mathcal{O}(n^{2/3}\log n^{\prime})}=2^{\mathcal{O}(n^{2/3}\log n)}.

This completes the proof.
∎

4
Hardness

In this section, we explore the
NP
-hardness of
MIS
in hereditary classes of ordered graphs.
We utilize four types of reductions, each presented in a dedicated section.
The last section summarizes the results and shows how they combine to prove the hardness part of
Theorem

1.1
.

4.1
Direct reduction from 3-
Sat

Figure 6:
The graph constructed in the proof of
Theorem

4.1
. Underscored segments indicate blocks, with the two literal vertices indicated by shading. Gray edges, contained within blocks, encode logical dependencies. Meanwhile the black ones join all occurrence vertices corresponding to some clause.

Theorem 4.1
.

MIS
is
NP
-hard when restricted to

-free ordered graphs.

Proof.

We reduce from the 3-
Sat
problem.
Consider an instance
\Phi
of 3-
Sat
with
n
variables and
m
clauses.
Denote by
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{C}
, respectively, the set of variables and the set of clauses of
\Phi
.
We can assume that each clause contains three distinct literals.
We create an ordered graph
G
in the following way – see also
Fig.

6
.

For each variable
x
, we create
literal
vertices
v(x)
and
v(\neg x)
representing, respectively, literals
x
and
\neg x
.
Next, for each clause
c
and each literal
\ell
occurring in
c
, we introduce an
occurrence
vertex
v(\ell,c)
.

Now let us define the edges of
G
– we split them into three types.
First, for every variable
x\in\mathcal{X}
, we add the edge
v(x)v(\neg x)
.
Next, for every literal
\ell
and every clause
c
containing the literal
\neg\ell
, we add the edge
v(\ell)v(\neg\ell,c)
.
Finally, for any clause
c
and any two literals
\ell,\ell^{\prime}
in
c
, we add the edge
v(\ell,c)v(\ell^{\prime},c)
.

We order the vertices of
G
as follows.
For each variable
x\in\mathcal{X}
, we create a block consisting of
v(x)
,
v(\neg x)
, and the vertices
v(x,c)
or
v(\neg x,c)
for every
c\in\mathcal{C}
that contains
x
.
The vertices of the block for
x
are ordered so that the first vertex is
v(x)
, followed by all the vertices
v(\neg x,c)
for
c\in\mathcal{C}
, followed by
v(\neg x)
, which is in turn followed by all
v(x,c)
’s for
c\in\mathcal{C}
.
The relative order of the vertices
v(\neg x,c)
(resp.,
v(x,c)
) is arbitrary.
Finally, the blocks corresponding to distinct variables are put one after another in arbitrary order.
This completes the construction of
G
, and it is clearly computable in polynomial time.

Let us verify that
G
has an independent set of size
n+m
if and only if
\Phi
is satisfiable.

Claim 4.1.1
.

If
\Phi
is satisfiable, then
\alpha(G)\geq n+m
.

Proof of Claim 4.1.1:

Suppose
\Phi
is satisfiable and let
\varphi
be a satisfying truth assignment. We construct an independent set
I
of size
n+m
as follows.
Whenever
\varphi(x)=\mathsf{true}
, we include
v(x)
in the set
I
; otherwise, we include
v(\neg x)
.
Since
\varphi
satisfies
\Phi
, each clause contains at least one literal set by
\varphi
to
\mathsf{true}
; we fix one such literal
\ell
arbitrarily for each clause
c\in\mathcal{C}
, and include the corresponding occurrence vertex
v(\ell,c)
in
I
.
Since such vertices are not adjacent to each other, nor to the literal vertices included previously,
I
is indeed an independent set.
Moreover, as
I
includes one vertex for each variable and one vertex for every clause,
|I|=n+m
, as desired.

\lrcorner

Claim 4.1.2
.

If
\alpha(G)\geq n+m
, then
\Phi
is satisfiable.

Proof of Claim 4.1.2:

First, let us observe that the vertices of
G
can be partitioned into
n+m
cliques, namely
n
copies of
K_{2}
of the form
\{v(x),v(\neg x)\}
corresponding to the variables of
\Phi
, and
m
triangles of the form
\{v(\ell_{1},c),v(\ell_{2},c),v(\ell_{3},c)\}
, corresponding to the clauses of
\Phi
.
Therefore, if
\alpha(G)\geq n+m
and
I
is an independent set of size
n+m
in
G
, then
I
has to contain precisely one vertex from each of these cliques.
We define a truth assignment
\varphi
satisfying
\Phi
as follows.
For each variable
x\in\mathcal{X}
, we set
\varphi(x)=\mathsf{true}
if
v(x)\in S
, otherwise we set
\varphi(x)=\mathsf{false}
.
Note that for every clause
c\in\mathcal{C}
and every literal
\ell
in
c
, the occurrence vertex
v(\ell,c)
is adjacent to the vertex
v(\neg\ell)
.
Furthermore,
v(\neg\ell)\notin I
if and only if
\varphi(\ell)=\mathsf{true}
,
and thus the vertex
v(\ell,c)
can be included in
I
only if
\varphi(\ell)=\mathsf{true}
.
Therefore, since for every clause
c\in\mathcal{C}
, there must be a literal
\ell
such that
v(\ell,c)\in I
, the assignment
\varphi
satisfies
\Phi
.
This completes the proof of the claim.

\lrcorner

It only remains to verify that
G
is

-free.

Claim 4.1.3
.

G
does not contain

as an induced subgraph.

Proof of Claim 4.1.3:

Suppose
G
contains an induced

; let
a,b,c,d
be its consecutive vertices.
Consider the vertex
a
. Since
a
is the leftmost vertex of the copy of

, only its right neighbors are relevant.
First suppose that
a
is a literal vertex.
Then, all right neighbors of
a
form a segment.
In particular, there is no vertex between
b
and
d
which is nonadjacent to
a
, which contradicts the fact that
c
is between
b
and
d
, and is nonadjacent to
a
.
So now suppose that
a
is an occurrence vertex, say
a=v(\ell_{1},c)
, for a literal
\ell_{1}
and a clause
c
.
Note that the only right neighbors of
a
can be the vertices
v(\ell_{2},c)
and
v(\ell_{3},c)
, where
c=\ell_{1}\lor\ell_{2}\lor\ell_{3}
.
But there is an edge
v(\ell_{2},c)v(\ell_{3},c)
in
G
, and
a
has two right neighbors
b,d
that are nonadjacent to each other, which is a contradiction.

\lrcorner

This completes the proof of the theorem.
∎

4.2
2-subdivision of every edge

In this section, we utilize Poljak’s subdivision trick, mentioned already in the Introduction.
A
2-subdivision of an edge
xy
in an unordered graph
G
is the operation of replacing
xy
with a four-vertex path with endpoints
x
and
y
(the internal vertices are new).
If
G^{\prime}
is a graph obtained from
G
by performing a 2-subdivision on a single edge, then
\alpha(G^{\prime})=\alpha(G)+1

[
43
]
.
Furthermore, if
G^{(2)}
is obtained from
G
by performing a 2-subdivision on each edge of
G
,
then
\alpha(G^{(2)})=\alpha(G)+|E(G)|
.
We call
G^{(2)}
a
2-subdivision of
G
.

Common outline of all reductions.

All reductions in this section have the same outline.
We reduce from the
MIS
problem in general unordered graphs.
We start with an arbitrary instance
(G,k)
and fix an arbitrary ordering of
G
, making it an ordered graph.
Then, we consider the 2-subdivision
G^{(2)}
of
G
and equip it with an order defined in a specific way, depending on the reduction.
By the observation above, we have that
\alpha(G)\geq k
if and only if
\alpha(G^{(2)})\geq k+|E(G)|
.

In order to complete the reduction, it suffices to define an ordering of
G^{(2)}
so that it belongs to the particular class.
In all reductions, the relative order of the vertices of
V(G^{(2)})\cap V(G)
(called
core
vertices) is the same as in
G
.
Thus, in the proofs, we will only discuss the placement of the remaining vertices (called
dummy
vertices).

Before we proceed to the reductions, let us introduce some terminology that will allow us to distinguish between the two dummy vertices corresponding to the same edge of
G
.
Let
e=xy
be an edge of
G
where
x\prec y
; note that their relative order is the same in
G
and in
G^{(2)}
.
In the construction of
G^{(2)}
, we replace
e
with the path
x-\ell_{e}-r_{e}-y
.
Now, the dummy vertex
\ell_{e}
is called the
left dummy
corresponding to
e
, while
r_{e}
is called the
right dummy
corresponding to
e
.

Let us also remark that in all reductions in this section we actually exclude certain graphs
H
as
subgraphs
, not only as induced subgraphs. Note that this is a stronger property, and thus the reductions are stronger as well. The graphs that exclude a graph
H
as a subgraph is referred to as
H
-subgraph-free
.
We also write
(
H_{1},H_{2},\ldots,H_{k}
)-subgraph-free
to denote the class of ordered graphs that are
H_{i}
-subgraph-free for every
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,k\}
.

Theorem 4.2
.

MIS
is
NP
-hard when restricted to (

,

,

,

,

,

,

,

)-subgraph-free ordered graphs.

Proof.

Let us define an appropriate ordering of the vertices of
G^{(2)}
, see also
Fig.

7
.
All core vertices precede all dummy vertices, whereas dummy vertices follow the lexicographic order of their respective edges, with left dummy being immediately followed by the right dummy corresponding to the same edge.
More formally, we stipulate that
\ell_{e}\prec r_{e}
for every edge
e
of
G
and
r_{e}\prec\ell_{f}
whenever
e\prec f
.

We now demonstrate that
G^{(2)}
does not contain any of the listed graphs as an ordered subgraph.

Claim 4.2.1
.

G^{(2)}
is (

,

,

)-subgraph-free.

Proof of Claim 4.2.1:

Suppose that
G^{(2)}
contains a copy of one of the graphs listed above as a subgraph, and let
a,b,c
be the consecutive vertices of

in that copy.

Since
c
has at least two left neighbors, it must be a right dummy
r_{uv}
of some edge
uv
of
G
, with
u\prec v
.
This means that its two (left) neighbors are precisely
v
and
\ell_{uv}
, with
v\prec\ell_{uv}
.
Therefore the vertices of

are
a=v,b=\ell_{uv},c=r_{uv}
.
In
G^{(2)}
, however, there is no vertex in between
\ell_{uv}
and
r_{uv}
, so it cannot contain

as a subgraph.
Moreover, the only neighbor of
\ell_{uv}
other than
r_{uv}
is the vertex
u\prec v
– thus, there can be no subgraph

or

in
G^{(2)}
.

\lrcorner

Claim 4.2.2
.

G^{(2)}
is (

,

,

,

)-subgraph-free.

Proof of Claim 4.2.2:

Suppose
G^{(2)}
contains a copy of one of the listed subgraphs, and let
a\prec b\preceq c\prec d\prec e
be the consecutive vertices of that copy.
Note that
b=c
precisely when the subgraph has four vertices.

Since
b
has a left neighbor, it must be a dummy vertex.
Therefore any vertex that follows
b
, i.e.,
c,d,e
, is a dummy as well.
However, the only edges between two dummy vertices in
G^{(2)}
connect vertices that immediately follow one another.
Thus
G^{(2)}
cannot contain

nor

as a subgraph.

Moreover, if
G^{(2)}
contains either

or

, then
d
is adjacent to a dummy vertex on either side.
But
G^{(2)}
has no vertices with that property, a contradiction.

\lrcorner

Claim 4.2.3
.

G^{(2)}
is

-subgraph-free.

Proof of Claim 4.2.3:

Suppose that
G^{(2)}
contains a copy of

, and let
a,b,c
be the consecutive vertices of

in that copy.

The vertex
b
, having both a left and a right neighbor, must be a left dummy of some edge
uv
of
G
with
u\prec v
.
Consequently, its left neighbor is
u
.
Consider any vertex
u^{\prime}\prec u
.
As it precedes a core vertex, it must itself be a core vertex.
For this reason, the only neighbors of
u^{\prime}
in
G^{(2)}
are dummy vertices corresponding to the edges of
G
adjacent to
u^{\prime}
.
As
u^{\prime}\prec u
, all of these edges precede
uv
in lexicographic order.
Thus all the neighbors of
u^{\prime}
in
G^{(2)}
precede
\ell_{uv}
, meaning a

subgraph cannot occur.

\lrcorner

This completes the proof. ∎

Theorem 4.3

,

.

More formally, we stipulate that
r_{e}\prec\ell_{e}
for each edge
e
and

whenever

.

Thus we have obtained an ordered graph

.
We now prove it does not contain any of the subgraphs listed in the theorem.

Claim 4.3.1
.

G^{(2)}
is (

,

,

)-subgraph-free.

, with

.

.

.

and

.

Thus all the neighbors of

in

, meaning a

\lrcorner

Theorem 4.4
.

MIS
is
NP
-hard when restricted to (

,

)-subgraph-free ordered graphs.

Proof.

Let us define an appropriate ordering of the vertices of
G^{(2)}
, see also
Fig.

9
.
The order of vertices is as follows: all right dummies precede all core vertices, which in turn precede all left dummies.
The relative order of the dummy vertices can be arbitrary.

Thus, we have obtained an ordered graph
G^{(2)}
. We proceed by showing it does not contain subgraphs

or

.

Claim 4.4.1
.

G^{(2)}
is

-subgraph-free.

Proof of Claim 4.4.1:

Suppose that
G^{(2)}
contains a copy of

as a subgraph, and let
a,b,c,d,e,f
be its consecutive vertices.

The vertex
b
has a left neighbor, so it must be either a core vertex or a left dummy.
Thus,
c
, must also be a core vertex or a left dummy, because
b\prec c
.
Then
d
has either a core vertex or a left dummy as a left neighbor, meaning it could only be a left dummy.
But in that case, both
e,f
are left dummies, precluding an edge between them.
This is a contradiction.

\lrcorner

Claim 4.4.2
.

G^{(2)}
is

-subgraph-free.

Proof of Claim 4.4.2:

Suppose that
G^{(2)}
contains a copy of

as a subgraph, and let
a,b,c,d
be its consecutive vertices.

The vertex
a
, having at least two right neighbors, is a right dummy
r_{uv}
for some
u\prec v
.
Its neighbors are, therefore, only
v
and
\ell_{uv}
, with
v\prec\ell_{uv}
, so
b=v
and
d=\ell_{uv}
.
Furthermore, the neighbors of
d=\ell_{uv}
are precisely
r_{uv}
and
u
, and thus
c=u
.
This, however, is a contradiction, as
u\prec v
by definition, whereas
b=v\prec u=c
in

.

\lrcorner

This completes the proof.

Theorem 4.5
.

MIS
is
NP
-hard when restricted to (

,

,

,

) -subgraph-free ordered graphs.

Proof.

Let us define an appropriate ordering of the vertices of
G^{(2)}
, see also
Fig.

10
.
The order of vertices is as follows: all core vertices, precede all the left dummies, in the lexicographic order of their corresponding edges, which are in turn followed by all the right dummies, again in the lexicographic order of their respective edges.
Thus we have obtained an ordered graph
G^{(2)}
.

Claim 4.5.1
.

G^{(2)}
is

-subgraph-free.

Proof of Claim 4.5.1:

Suppose that
G^{(2)}
contains a copy of

as a subgraph, and let
a,b,c,d
be the consecutive vertices of

in that copy.

Since
b
has a left neighbor, it is not a core vertex.
Neither is
c
, as it follows
b
.
Moreover,
c
has a right neighbor, so it must be the left dummy
\ell_{e}
of some edge
e
of
G
.
Therefore
d
is the right dummy
r_{e}
.
Observe that
b
is followed by a left dummy, so it is not itself a right dummy, i.e.,
b=\ell_{f}
for some edge
f=uv
with
u\prec v
.
Furthermore, as
a
is a left neighbor of
b
, it holds that
a=u
.
Therefore, the vertices of

are precisely
a=u,b=\ell_{f},c=\ell_{e},d=r_{e}
.
Moreover, since
\ell_{f}\prec\ell_{e}
, it holds
f\prec e
.

Now consider any vertex
u^{\prime}\prec u
and let
g
be an edge incident to
u^{\prime}
.
Clearly, the left endpoint of
g
precedes
u
, and thus
g\prec f\prec e
.
Therefore
\ell_{g}\prec r_{g}\prec r_{e}
, so all the neighbors of
u^{\prime}
in
G^{(2)}
precede
r_{e}
.
Consequently,
G^{(2)}
cannot contain

as a subgraph, which completes the proof of the claim.

\lrcorner

Claim 4.5.2
.

G^{(2)}
is (

,

)-subgraph-free.

Proof of Claim 4.5.2:

Suppose that
G^{(2)}
contains a copy of one of the graphs listed in the claim as a subgraph, and let
a,b,c
be the consecutive vertices of

in that copy.

The vertex
c
, having two left neighbors, is a right dummy
r_{uv}
of some edge
uv
with
u\prec v
.
Moreover, the remaining vertices of

are the only neighbors of
r_{uv}
, i.e.,
a=v
and
b=\ell_{uv}
.
Because
r_{uv}
can only be followed by other right dummies, and those form an independent set, a subgraph

cannot occur in
G^{(2)}
.

Moreover, suppose that
G^{(2)}
contains an edge
xy
with
v\prec x\prec y\prec\ell_{uv}
.
As there are no edges between core vertices or between left dummies, we have
y=\ell_{xv^{\prime}}
, where
x\prec v^{\prime}
, and
x
and
v^{\prime}
are two core vertices.
However, as the left dummies follow the lexicographic order of their corresponding edges and
y=\ell_{xv^{\prime}}\prec\ell_{uv}
, we have
x\preceq u\prec v
, which is a contradiction with the choice of
xy
.

\lrcorner

Claim 4.5.3
.

G^{(2)}
is

-free.

Proof of Claim 4.5.3:

Suppose that
G^{(2)}
contains a copy of

as a subgraph, and let
a,b,c,d,e,f
be its consecutive vertices.

The vertex
b
has a left neighbor, so it must be a dummy.
Thus
c
, must also be a dummy, because
b\prec c
.
Then
d
has a dummy as a left neighbor, meaning it could only be a right dummy.
But in that case, both
e,f
are right dummies, precluding an edge between them.
This is a contradiction.

\lrcorner

This completes the proof.
∎

4.3
Long subdivision

In this section, we again take advantage of Poljak’s subdivision trick, albeit in another way.

Theorem 4.6
.

MIS
is
NP
-hard on (

,

) -subgraph-free ordered graphs and on

-subgraph-free ordered graphs.

Proof.

We will provide two parallel reductions: one for (

,

)-subgraph-free case and another for

-subgraph-free case. The two reductions are very similar, and the main difference is in the way we order the vertices of the constructed graph.

Let
(G,k)
be an instance of
MIS
, and let us fix an arbitrary ordering on
V(G)
.
We will construct two ordered graphs
G_{1}
,
G_{2}
and integers
k_{1},k_{2}
such that: (i)
\alpha(G_{1})\geq k_{1}
if and only if
\alpha(G)\geq k
, (ii)
\alpha(G_{2})\geq k_{2}
if and only if
\alpha(G)\geq k
, (iii)
G_{1}
is (

,

)-subgraph-free, and (iv)
G_{2}
is

-subgraph-free.
The construction of
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
is performed in two steps; most of the parts of the constructions are common to
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
. See also
Fig.

11
.

Step 1.

As an intermediate step we construct two ordered graphs
G_{1}^{\prime}
and
G_{2}^{\prime}
– these graphs have the same underlying graph, but different ordering.
Let us enumerate the edges of
G
according to their lexicographic order; for an edge
e\in E(G)
, let
\pi(e)
denote its position, i.e., the number of edges preceding
e
plus one.
For every edge
e=uv\in E(G)
, where
u\prec v
, let us doubly subdivide it
\pi(e)
times, replacing
e
with the path
u-u_{1,e}-u_{2,e}-\ldots-u_{\pi(e),e}-v_{\pi(e),e}-\ldots-v_{2,e}-v_{1,e}-v
.
Next, we partition the expanded set of vertices into sets
V_{0},V_{1},V_{2},\ldots,V_{|E(G)|}
according to their indices, namely by stipulating that
V_{0}=V(G)
, and for
e=uv\in E(G)
,
i\in[|E(G)|]
, we have
u_{i,e},v_{i,e}\in V_{i}
.
We call the sets
V_{0},V_{1},V_{2},\ldots,V_{|E(G)|}

layers
.

Now the order of the vertices of
V_{0}\cup\ldots\cup V_{|E(G)|}
is slightly different in
G_{1}^{\prime}
and
G_{2}^{\prime}
. In both cases, we order the vertices so that for
i<j
, we have
V_{i}\prec V_{j}
.
Furthermore, in
G_{1}^{\prime}
, the order among vertices in each
V_{i}
is such that:
u_{i,e}\prec v_{i,f}
whenever
u\prec v
in
G
, and
u_{i,e}\prec u_{i,f}
whenever
e\prec f
lexicographically in
G
.
In
G_{2}^{\prime}
, the order among vertices in each
V_{i}
depends on the parity of
i
: (i) if
i
is even, we stipulate that
u_{i,e}\prec v_{i,f}
whenever
u\prec v
in
G
and
u_{i,e}\prec u_{i,f}
whenever
e\prec f
in
G
, and (ii) if
i
is odd, we order the vertices in
V_{i}
so that
u_{i,e}\prec v_{i,f}
whenever
v\prec u
in
G
and
u_{i,e}\prec u_{i,f}
whenever
f\prec e
in
G
.
This completes the construction of
G_{1}^{\prime}
and
G_{2}^{\prime}
.
We proceed to the second step.

Step 2.

Let
p\in\{1,2\}
. For every
e=uv\in E(G)
, where
u\prec v
, we proceed as follows.
Consider the segment of
G_{p}^{\prime}
with endpoints
u_{\pi(e),e}
and
v_{\pi(e),e}
– we point out that by the construction, there is an edge
u_{\pi(e),e}v_{\pi(e),e}
.
We again aim to subdivide this edge an even number of times in order to avoid the forbidden subgraphs.

Let
\{x_{0},\ldots,x_{k}\}
be the vertex set of the segment so that
u_{\pi(e),e}=x_{0}\prec x_{1}\prec x_{2}\prec\ldots\prec x_{k}=v_{\pi(e),e}
, where
k\in\mathbb{N}
is the length of the segment.
For each
i\in\{0,\ldots,k-1\}
, we create a vertex
\omega_{i,e}
, and we insert it in the order so that
x_{i}\prec\omega_{i,e}\prec x_{i+1}
.
Additionally, if
k
is odd, we create another vertex
\omega_{k,e}
with
\omega_{k-1,e}\prec\omega_{k,e}\prec v_{\pi(e),e}
.
Let
\Omega_{e}
be the set of vertices created this way, together with
x_{0}
and
x_{k}
.
Note that we have ensured that
\Omega_{e}
has even size.
We replace the edge
x_{0}x_{k}
with the path
x_{0}-\omega_{0,e}-\omega_{1,e}-\ldots-\omega_{k-1,e}-(\omega_{k,e}-)x_{k}
.
This completes the construction of
G_{p}
.

Correctness.

Observe that
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
were constructed from
G
by some numbers
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
, respectively, of double subdivisions.
Let
k_{1}=k+\ell_{1}
and
k_{2}=k+\ell_{2}
.
As we already mentioned in the previous section, it holds that
\alpha(G)\geq k
if and only if
\alpha(G_{1})\geq k_{1}
, and
\alpha(G)\geq k
if and only if
\alpha(G_{2})\geq k_{2}
, as desired.
Moreover, the number of double subdivisions performed in the first step is
\mathcal{O}(m^{2})
, and
\mathcal{O}(n\cdot m)
in the second, where
n,m
are, respectively, the number of vertices and edges of
G
.
Therefore, the instances
(G_{1},k_{1})
and
(G_{2},k_{2})
are constructed in polynomial time.
It remains to prove that
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
do not contain forbidden subgraphs.

We first divide the edges of
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
into two types: the edges that join a vertex from some
V_{i}
with a vertex in
V_{i+1}
– we will call those
long
edges; the others that join pairs of vertices in some
\Omega_{e}
– those we will call
short
edges.
The following observation about short edges in both
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
is immediate from the construction.

Claim 4.6.1
.

If
xy
is a short edge, then there is at most one vertex
z\neq x,y
in the segment
xy
.
Moreover,
z
belongs to some
V_{i}
, and not to any
\Omega_{e}
, and thus
z
is not an endpoint of another short edge.

Furthermore, we have the following property of the long edges in
G_{1}
.

Claim 4.6.2
.

Let
xy
and
x^{\prime}y^{\prime}
be long edges of
G_{1}
such that
x\prec y
,
x^{\prime}\prec y^{\prime}
, and
x\prec x^{\prime}
, and all vertices
x,x^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}
are distinct. Then
y\prec y^{\prime}
.

Proof of Claim 4.6.2:

Suppose that there are long edges
xy
,
x^{\prime}y^{\prime}
such that
x\prec y
,
x^{\prime}\prec y^{\prime}
, and
x\prec x^{\prime}
.
Since
xy
is long, there is
i\in\{0,\ldots,|E(G)|-1\}
such that
x\in V_{i}
and
y\in V_{i+1}
.
Moreover, as
x^{\prime}y^{\prime}
is long and we have
x\prec x^{\prime}\prec y^{\prime}\preceq y
, it must hold that
x^{\prime}\in V_{i}
and
y^{\prime}\in V_{i+1}
.
Recall that by the construction of
V_{i}
, there are
e=uv\in E(G)
and
f=u^{\prime}v^{\prime}\in E(G)
such that
x=u_{i,e}
,
y=u_{i+1,e}
,
x^{\prime}=u^{\prime}_{i,f}
, and
y^{\prime}=u^{\prime}_{i+1,f}
.
Since
u_{i,e}\prec u^{\prime}_{i,f}
, we either have
u\prec u^{\prime}
in
G
or
u=u^{\prime}
and
e
precedes
f
in the lexicographic order.
In both cases, this implies that
y\prec y^{\prime}
, as desired.

\lrcorner

Now we are ready to prove that
G_{1}
does not contain forbidden subgraphs.

Claim 4.6.3
.

G_{1}
is

-subgraph-free.

Proof of Claim 4.6.3:

Suppose there is a subgraph

in
G_{1}
.
By
4.6.1
, both its edges are long.
But
4.6.2
precludes long edges from inducing an

, a contradiction.

\lrcorner

Claim 4.6.4
.

G_{1}
is

-subgraph-free.

Proof of Claim 4.6.4:

Suppose there is such a subgraph in
G_{1}
.
By
4.6.1
, the edge joining the first vertex and the last is long, and then by
4.6.2
the remaining two are short.
But this contradicts the second part of
4.6.1
, as short edges cannot induce an

.

\lrcorner

It remains to prove that
G_{2}
is

-subgraph-free. We again first prove the following observation on the long edges in
G_{2}
.

Claim 4.6.5
.

Let
xy
and
x^{\prime}y^{\prime}
be long edges of
G_{2}
such that
x
and
x^{\prime}
belong to the same layer, and
x\prec y
,
x^{\prime}\prec y^{\prime}
, and
x\prec x^{\prime}
, and all vertices
x,x^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}
are distinct. Then
y^{\prime}\prec y
.

Proof of Claim 4.6.5:

Let
i\in\{0,\ldots,|E(G)|-1\}
be such that
x,x^{\prime}\in V_{i}
, and suppose there are long edges
xy
and
x^{\prime}y^{\prime}
such that
x\prec y
,
x^{\prime}\prec y^{\prime}
, and
x\prec x^{\prime}
, and all vertices
x,x^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}
are distinct.
Since
xy
and
x^{\prime}y^{\prime}
are long, we have
y,y^{\prime}\in V_{i+1}
.
Recall that by the construction, there are
e=uv\in E(G)
and
f=u^{\prime}v^{\prime}\in E(G)
such that
x=u_{i,e}
,
y=u_{i+1,e}
,
x^{\prime}=u^{\prime}_{i,f}
, and
y^{\prime}=u^{\prime}_{i+1,f}
.
Since
u_{i,e}\prec u^{\prime}_{i,f}
, we either have
u\prec u^{\prime}
in
G
or
u=u^{\prime}
and
e\prec f
lexicographically.
As
i
and
i+1
have opposite parity, in both cases this implies that
y^{\prime}\prec y
, as desired.
\lrcorner

We are ready to prove that
G_{2}
is

-subgraph-free.

Claim 4.6.6
.

G_{2}
is

-subgraph-free.

Proof of Claim 4.6.6:

Suppose that
G_{2}
contains a copy of

as a subgraph, and let
a,b,c,d,e,f
be its consecutive vertices.
By
4.6.1
, all edges of

are long.
Let
a\in V_{i}
.
This implies
d\in V_{i+1}
.
By
4.6.5
applied to edges
ad
and
be
, we have
b\notin V_{i}
.
Furthermore, since
a\prec b\prec d
, it holds that
b\in V_{i+1}
.
Now
c
, being both preceded and followed by some vertex from
V_{i+1}
, must itself belong to
V_{i+1}
.
This, however, implies that the edges
be
and
cf
violate
4.6.5
, a contradiction that proves the claim.

\lrcorner

This completes the proof.
∎

Figure 11:
A layer
V_{e}
, containing the path
\Omega_{e}
, in
G_{1}
(up) and
G_{2}
(down) as in the proof of
Theorem

4.6
.

Train reduction

.
The constructions we use are more involved than those we have presented so far; they are inspired by the
NP
-hardness proof for
List 4-Coloring
in

-free graphs
[
41
]
.

We organize this Section into three parts.
Our aim is to reduce
MIS
in general graphs to
MIS
in
H
-free ordered graphs.
First, in
Section

4.4.1
, we introduce the main building blocks in our reductions –
trains
,
coupling
, and
permutation gadgets
.
Next, in
Section

4.4.2
we show how to use these building blocks in order to obtain the desired reductions.
Finally, in
Section

4.4.3
we present the construction of permutation gadgets, completing the proof.

4.4.1
Building blocks

The main component of our constructions is a
train
.

Definition 4.7

(Train)
.

For integers
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\geq 0
,
a
train
is a tuple

\mathcal{T}=(T,(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}),(y_{1},y_{2},\ldots y_{\ell^{\prime}})),

where
T
is an ordered graph, such that

1.

the vertices
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell},y_{1},y_{2},\ldots y_{\ell^{\prime}}
are pairwise distinct,

2.

x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}
are, in that order, a prefix of
T
,

3.

y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{\ell^{\prime}}
are, in that order, a suffix of
T
,

4.

both
\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\}
and
\{y_{1},y_{2},\ldots y_{\ell^{\prime}}\}
are independent sets.

We call
(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell})
(resp.,
(y_{1},y_{2},\ldots y_{\ell^{\prime}})
’s) the
input vertices
(resp.,
output vertices
) of the train
\mathcal{T}
.
By the
underlying graph
of
\mathcal{T}
we mean that of
T
.
For an ordered graph
H
, we say that
\mathcal{T}
is
H
-free if
T
is
H
-free.

Coupling and avoidability.

Consider two trains

\mathcal{T}^{1}=(T^{1},(x_{1}^{1},x_{2}^{1},\ldots,x_{\ell}^{1}),(y_{1}^{1},y_{2}^{1},\ldots,y_{\ell^{\prime}}^{1}))\text{ \ and \ }\mathcal{T}^{2}=(T^{2},(x_{1}^{2},x_{2}^{2},\ldots,x_{\ell^{\prime}}^{2}),(y_{1}^{2},y_{2}^{2},\ldots,y_{\ell^{\prime\prime}}^{2}))

on disjoint sets of vertices.
Let
T
be the ordered graph obtained from
T^{1}
and
T^{2}
by identifying
y_{i}^{1}
with
x_{i}^{2}
for each
i\in[\ell^{\prime}]
.
The train
\mathcal{T}=(T,(x_{1}^{1},x_{2}^{1},\ldots,x_{\ell}^{1}),(y_{1}^{2},y_{2}^{2},\ldots y_{\ell^{\prime\prime}}^{2}))
will be called the
coupling
of
\mathcal{T}^{1}
and
\mathcal{T}^{2}
.
The same name will also denote the operation of obtaining
\mathcal{T}
from its component trains.
Clearly,
\mathcal{T}
satisfies properties
1
,
2
,
3
, and
4
of
Definition

4.7
.

However, it might happen that the coupling of two
H
-free trains is not
H
-free itself.
We say that an ordered graph
H
is
avoidable
if the coupling of any two
H
-free trains is
H
-free.

Let us introduce a criterion for avoidability.

Lemma 4.8
.

Suppose
H
is an ordered graph containing (not necessarily distinct) vertices
a,b,c,d,e,f
, in that order, such that:

1.

a
is the first and
f
the last vertex of
H
;

2.

H
contains edges
ae,bf,cd
.

Then
H
is avoidable.

Proof.

Let

\mathcal{T}^{1}=(T^{1},(x_{1}^{1},x_{2}^{1},\ldots,x_{\ell}^{1}),(y_{1}^{1},y_{2}^{1},\ldots,y_{\ell^{\prime}}^{1}))
and
\mathcal{T}^{2}=(T^{2},(x_{1}^{2},x_{2}^{2},\ldots,x_{\ell^{\prime}}^{2}),(y_{1}^{2},y_{2}^{2},\ldots,y_{\ell^{\prime\prime}}^{2}))
be two
H
-free trains and let
\mathcal{T}
be their coupling.
For contradiction, suppose that
\mathcal{T}
contains an induced copy of
H
.
Since
\mathcal{T}^{1}
is
H
-free,
f\in V(T^{2})\setminus V(T^{1})
.
Because of the edge
bf
, we have
b\in V(T^{2})
.
Similarly,
a,e\in V(T^{1})
.
Now, as
b\preceq c\preceq d\preceq e
, it must hold that
c,d\in V(T^{1})\cap V(T^{2})
.
However, the set
V(T^{1})\cap V(T^{2})
is independent, contradicting the existence of the edge
cd
.
∎

Notably, this implies the avoidability of both

and

.
Indeed, letting
a=b
and
e=f
yields

, a subgraph of

.
In case of

we apply
Lemma

4.8
with all vertices distinct.

Interchangeability.

Let us start with a definition of an operation that is similar in spirit to Poljak’s subdivision trick, i.e., a local replacement of certain induced subgraph in a graph that preserves the independence number.
The definitions below concern unordered graphs.

A
boundaried graph

(G,B)
is a graph with distinguished subset of vertices
B
called the
boundary
.
Two boundaried graphs
(G_{1},B_{1})
and
(G_{2},B_{2})
are
compatible
if
V(G_{1})\cap V(G_{2})=B_{1}=B_{2}
and
G_{1}[B_{1}]=G_{2}[B_{2}]
.
Let
G
be a graph that contains a boundaried graph
(G_{1},B)
as an induced subgraph such that
N(V(G_{1})\setminus B)\subseteq B
.
By
replacing

(G_{1},B)
with a compatible boundaried graph
(G_{2},B)
we mean removing all vertices from
V(G_{1})\setminus B
and adding the vertices from
V(G_{2})\setminus B
together with all edges of
G_{2}
incident to these vertices.

Definition 4.9

(Interchangeability)
.

Let
(G_{1},B)
,
(G_{2},B)
be two compatible boundaried graphs.
We say that
(G_{1},B)
and
(G_{2},B)
are
interchangeable
if for every graph
G
, the following holds.

(
\star
)

Suppose that
G
contains an induced copy of
G_{1}
such that
N(V(G_{1})\setminus B)\subseteq B
, and let
G^{\prime}
be obtained from
G
by replacing
(G_{1},B)
with
(G_{2},B)
.
Then
\alpha(G)=\alpha(G^{\prime})
.

Permutation gadgets.

We are ready to introduce the crucial blocks (or rather, carriages) of our reductions in this section.

Definition 4.10
.

Let
\ell\geq 1
be an integer and let
\sigma:[\ell]\to[\ell]
be a permutation.
A
permutation gadget for
\sigma
is a pair
(\mathcal{P},\overline{k})
,
where
\mathcal{P}=(P,(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}),(y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{\ell}))
is a train
and
\overline{k}=(k^{1},\ldots,k^{\ell})
is a vector of
\ell
positive integers, all of equal parity, such that the following holds:

Let
L
be the (unordered) linear forest consisting of
\ell
paths
L^{1},L^{2},\ldots,L^{\ell}
, where
L^{i}
is a path with endpoints
x_{i}
and
y_{\sigma(i)}
and with
k^{i}
vertices in total. Then,
(L,\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}\{x_{i},y_{i}\})
is interchangeable with
(P,\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}\{x_{i},y_{i}\})
,
where in the latter we treat
P
as an unordered graph.

Whenever
\overline{k}
is clear from the context, we will simply say that
\mathcal{P}
is a permutation gadget for
\sigma
.

In the following lemma we show that for
H\in\{\hbox to24.18pt{\vbox to8.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.70703pt\lower-0.70703pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

, such
H
-free permutation gadgets exist.

Lemma 4.11
.

For every
\ell
, and for every permutation
\sigma:[\ell]\rightarrow[\ell]
there exist:

1.

a

-free permutation gadget for
\sigma
;

2.

a

-free permutation gadget for
\sigma
.

Moreover, both gadgets can be constructed in time polynomial in
\ell
.

4.4.2
Hardness reduction

We postpone the proof of
Lemma

4.11
to
Section

4.4.3
, and now we show how it implies the desired hardness results.

Theorem 4.12
.

MIS
is
NP
-hard when restricted to

-free ordered graphs and to

-free ordered graphs.

Proof.

Let
(G,k)
be an instance of
MIS
and let
m
be the number of edges of
G
.

We aim to construct ordered graphs
G_{1}
,
G_{2}
and integers
k_{1},k_{2}
such that: (i)
G_{1}
is

-free, (ii)
G_{2}
is

-free, (iii)
\alpha(G_{1})\geq k_{1}
if and only if
\alpha(G)\geq k
, and (iv)
\alpha(G_{2})\geq k_{2}
if and only if
\alpha(G)\geq k
.
Most parts of the construction of
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
will be common, so we will describe them together.

Construction of
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
.

Each of the graphs
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
is obtained by the coupling of three trains.

The first one is the same for both
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
and it is called the
locomotive
(see
Fig.

12
).
We start with introducing the set
V(G)
, which we order the arbitrarily.
Then, we define the set
V_{E}
so that for every
v\in V(G)
, and for every
e\in E(G)
such that
v
is an endpoint of
e
, we add to
V_{E}
the vertex
v_{e}
, adjacent to
v
.
Note that the graph constructed so far is similar to the 2-subdivision of
G
discussed in previous sections, except we do not add any edges between dummy vertices.
We place the vertices from
V_{E}
after the set
V(G)
, and the order inside
V_{E}
is such that
u_{e}\prec v_{f}
whenever
u\prec v
and the order between
v_{e}
and
v_{f}
is arbitrary.
The input of the locomotive is empty, while the output consists of the vertices from
V_{E}
.
Note that
|V_{E}|=2m
.

The second train is a permutation gadget for an appropriate permutation
\sigma
of
[2m]
.
For each
v_{e}\in V_{E}
, let
\pi(v_{e})\in[2m]
denote the position of
v_{e}
in
V_{E}
.
Let
\sigma:[2m]\to[2m]
be a permutation such that,
for every
e=uv\in E(G)
,
there is
i\in[2m-1]
such that
\{\sigma(\pi(u_{e})),\sigma(\pi(v_{e}))\}=\{i,i+1\}
.
We invoke
Lemma

4.11
to construct a

-free (in the case of
G_{1}
) or a

-free (in the case of
G_{2}
) permutation gadget
(\mathcal{P},\overline{k})
for
\sigma
,
where
\mathcal{P}=(P,(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{2m}),(y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{2m}))
.

Now let us proceed to the third (and final) train, called
caboose
(see
Fig.

12
).
It contains vertices
y_{1},\ldots,y_{2m}
as the input.
Next, we introduce the vertices
z_{1},\ldots,z_{2m}
, which we place in that order after the vertex
y_{2m}
.
For every
j\in[m]
, we add the edge
z_{2j-1}z_{2j}
.
In the construction of
G_{1}
, we add the edge
y_{i}z_{i}
for every
i\in[2m]
.
In the construction of
G_{2}
, we add the edge
y_{i}z_{2m+1-i}
instead. The output of the caboose is empty.

Now,
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are both obtained by coupling of the locomotive,
the permutation gadget
\mathcal{P}
, and the caboose.
This completes the construction of
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
.
Clearly,
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are constructed in polynomial time.

H
-freeness: properties (i) and (ii).

Let us verify that
G_{1}
is

-free and
G_{2}
is

-free.
As both

and

are avoidable, it is sufficient to verify that each of the three trains we used in the construction of
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
is, respectively,

-free or

-free.

It is straightforward to verify that the locomotive is even

-free,
so in particular

-free and

-free.
The corresponding property of the permutation gadget follows directly from
Lemma

4.11
.

Thus, let us focus on the caboose.
For
G_{1}
, notice that the middle edge of every copy of

in this train has to be contained in
\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{2m}\}
. This means that the endvertices of this edge must be consecutive so, in particular, this train is

-free.
In the case of
G_{2}
, the caboose does not even contain

, so it is in particular

-free.

Thus,
G_{1}
is

-free and
G_{2}
is

-free, as claimed.

Equivalence of instances: properties (iii) and (iv).

It remains to define
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
and prove the equivalence of the instances.
We show the case of
G_{1}
; the case of
G_{2}
is analogous.
Consider the permutation gadget
(\mathcal{P},\overline{k})
used in the construction, where
\overline{k}=(k^{1},\ldots,k^{2m})
.
Let us define
B=\bigcup_{i=1}^{2m}\{x_{i},y_{i}\}
.

Let
L
be the linear forest, as
Definition

4.10
, and denote by
L^{1},L^{2},\ldots,L^{2m}
its paths, so that
L^{i}
has
k^{i}
vertices and joins
x_{i}
with
y_{\sigma(i)}
.
By the definition of
\mathcal{P}
and
L
, we know that that
(P,B)
is interchangeable with
(L,B)
.
Let
G^{\prime}_{1}
be the unordered graph obtained from the underlying graph of
G_{1}
by replacing
(P,B)
with
(L,B)
.
Since
(P,B)
and
(L,B)
are interchangeable, we have
\alpha(G_{1})=\alpha(G_{1}^{\prime})
.

We claim that
G_{1}^{\prime}
can be equivalently obtained from
G
by subdividing each edge some even number of times.
Consider an edge
e=uv
of
G
.
Let
i=\pi(u_{e})
and
j=\pi(v_{e})
, and by symmetry assume that
\sigma(i)<\sigma(j)
, i.e.,
\sigma(j)=\sigma(i)+1
.
In
G^{\prime}_{1}
, the edge
e
is corresponds to the path:

u-\underbrace{u_{e}-\ldots-y_{\sigma(i)}}_{\text{$L^{i}$, i.e., $k^{i}$ vertices}}-z_{\sigma(i)}-z_{\sigma(j)}-\underbrace{y_{\sigma(j)}-\ldots-v_{e}}_{{}_{\text{$L^{j}$, i.e., $k^{j}$ vertices}}}-v,

In total, this path has
k^{i}+k^{j}+4
vertices, which is an even number as
k^{i}
and
k^{j}
have the same parity.
Thus, in the construction of
G_{1}^{\prime}
from
G
, the edge was doubly subdivided
k_{e}:=(k^{i}+k^{j}+2)/2
times.

Since the edge
uv
was chosen arbitrarily, we conclude that
G^{\prime}_{1}
was obtained by applying Poljak’s subdivision trick to
G
the total of

\sum_{e\in E(G)}k_{e},

times, and hence we have
\alpha(G_{1})=\alpha(G_{1}^{\prime})=\alpha(G)+\sum_{e\in E(G)}k_{e}
.
Setting
k_{1}=k+\sum_{e\in E(G)}k_{e}
proves (iii).
The arguments for (iv) are analogous, so the proof is complete.
∎

Figure 12:
The locomotive and two cabooses used in the proof of
Theorem

4.12
. In this and subsequent figures, underscoring indicates the input and output vertices.

4.4.3
Constructing permutation gadgets

It remains to prove
Lemma

4.11
.
We start with the following construction (see
Fig.

13
).

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
be graphs defined as follows:

\displaystyle V(G_{1})=\{a,b_{1},c,d,e_{1},f_{1},g\},

\displaystyle E(G_{1})=\{ab_{1},b_{1}c,de_{1},e_{1}f_{1},f_{1}g\},

\displaystyle V(G_{2})=\{a,b_{2},b_{2}^{\prime},c,d,e_{2},f_{2},g\},

\displaystyle E(G_{2})=\{ab_{2},b_{2}c,ab_{2}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime}c,b_{2}b_{2}^{\prime},de_{2},e_{2}f_{2},f_{2}g,b_{2}e_{2},b_{2}^{\prime}f_{2}\},

and let
B=V(G_{1})\cap V(G_{2})=\{a,c,d,g\}
.
Then,
(G_{1},B)
and
(G_{2},B)
are interchangeable.

Proof.

Let
G
be an arbitrary graph containing an induced copy of
G_{1}
, with
N(V(G_{1})\setminus B)\subseteq B
, and let
G^{\prime}
be the graph obtained by replacing this copy with
G_{2}
.

First, observe that
G_{2}
contains a subgraph isomorphic to
G_{1}
:
indeed, removing the vertex
b_{2}^{\prime}
and, additionally, the edge
b_{2}e_{2}
, yields a graph isomorphic to
G_{1}
.
Therefore,
G^{\prime}
also contains a copy of
G
as a subgraph, and the corresponding mapping
\varphi:V(G)\to V(G^{\prime})
of vertices is as follows:

\varphi(v)=\begin{cases}v&\text{if }v\in V(G)\setminus\{b_{1},e_{1},f_{1}\}\\
b_{2}&\text{if }v=b_{1}\\
e_{2}&\text{if }v=e_{1}\\
f_{2}&\text{if }v=f_{1}.\end{cases}

Claim 4.13.1
.

It holds that
\alpha(G)\geq\alpha(G^{\prime})
.

Proof of Claim 4.13.1:

Let
I^{\prime}
be an independent set in
G^{\prime}
, we will find an independent set
I
in
G
of the same size.
If
b_{2}^{\prime}\notin I^{\prime}
, we set
I=\{\varphi^{-1}(v)~|~v\in I^{\prime}\}
.
Thus, suppose that
b_{2}^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}
. In particular, this means that
a,b_{2},c\notin I^{\prime}
.
We can we set
I=\{\varphi^{-1}(v)~|~v\in I^{\prime}\setminus\{b_{2}^{\prime}\}\}\cup\{b_{2}\}
.
It is straightforward to verify that in both cases
I
is independent in
G
and that
|I|=|I^{\prime}|
, as desired.

\lrcorner

Claim 4.13.2
.

It holds that
\alpha(G)\leq\alpha(G^{\prime})
.

Proof of Claim 4.13.2:

Let
I
be an independent set in
G
, we will find an independent set
I^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}
of the same size.
If
I
contains at most one of
b_{1},e_{1}
, then we set
I^{\prime}=\{\varphi(v)~|~v\in I\}
.
Thus, suppose that
I
contains both
b_{1}
and
e_{1}
. In particular, this means that
a,c,d,f_{1}\notin I
.
We can we set
I^{\prime}=\{\varphi(v)~|~v\in I\setminus\{b_{1},e_{1}\}\}\cup\{b_{2}^{\prime},e_{2}\}
.
Again, it is straightforward to verify that in both cases
I^{\prime}
is independent in
G^{\prime}
and that
|I^{\prime}|=|I|
, as desired.

\lrcorner

Combining
4.13.2
and
4.13.1
, we obtain the desired equality, which completes the proof.
∎

a
b_{1}
c
d
e_{1}
f_{1}
g
\rightarrow
a
b_{2}
b_{2}^{\prime}
c
d
e_{2}
f_{2}
g

Figure 13:
An interchangeable pair described in
Lemma

4.13
.
G_{2}
can be viewed, up to isomorphism, as
G_{1}
augmented by the orange vertex and edges. The boundary vertices are
a,c,d,g
and only they might have neighbors outside the depicted subgraph.

Now we proceed to construct permutation gadgets.
The following observation is straightforward.

Observation 4.14
.

Let
\ell\geq 1
and let
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}
be permutations of
[\ell]
. Let
(\mathcal{P}_{\sigma},\overline{k}_{\sigma})
and
(\mathcal{P}_{\sigma^{\prime}},\overline{k}_{\sigma^{\prime}})
be, respectively, permutation gadgets for
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
.
Let
\mathcal{P}
be the coupling of
\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\sigma^{\prime}}

and let
\overline{k}=(k_{\sigma}^{i}+k_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{\sigma(i)}-1)_{i=1}^{\ell}
.
Then,
(\mathcal{P},\overline{k})
is a permutation gadget for
\sigma^{\prime}\circ\sigma
.

By
Lemma

4.8
and
4.14
, it is sufficient to construct gadgets for some basic permutations and combine them via coupling.
We say that a permutation
\sigma:[\ell]\to[\ell]
is an
elementary swap
if it exchanges two consecutive elements, i.e., there is
i\in[\ell-1]
such that for
j\in[\ell]\setminus\{i,i+1\}
, we have
\sigma(j)=j
, and
\sigma(i)=i+1
and
\sigma(i+1)=i
.

It is well-known that any permutation can be obtained as a composition of
\mathcal{O}(\ell^{2})
elementary swaps – this corresponds to the fact that the worst-case complexity of bubble sort is
\mathcal{O}(\ell^{2})
.
Thus, for

, it is sufficient to construct

Proof of
Lemma

4.11
for elementary swaps.

Case:
H=

.

Let
\ell\geq 2
and let
j\in[\ell-1]
and
\sigma
be an elementary swap that exchanges elements
j
with
j+1
.

We will construct a

-free permutation gadget
(\mathcal{P},\overline{k})
,
where
\mathcal{P}=(P,(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}),(y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{\ell}))
is a train.
The vertex set of
P
consists of the following four
canonical
segments of vertices, in the described order:

V_{0}

containing vertices
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}
(which are the input of the gadget),

V_{1}

consisting of vertices
x_{1}^{1},x_{2}^{1},\ldots,x_{j-1}^{1},u,v,x_{j}^{1},x^{1}_{j+1},\ldots,x_{\ell}^{1}
,

V_{2}

consisting of vertices
x_{1}^{2},x_{2}^{2},\ldots,x_{j-1}^{2},w,x_{j+1}^{2},x_{j}^{2},x_{j+2}^{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}^{2}
,

V_{3}

consisting of vertices
x_{1}^{3},x_{2}^{3},\ldots,x_{j-1}^{3},x_{j+1}^{3},x_{j}^{3},x_{j+2}^{3},

\ldots,x_{\ell}^{3}
(which are the output of the gadget, we identify them with corresponding vertices
y_{i}
).

We connect the vertices by creating paths
x_{i}-x_{i}^{1}-x_{i}^{2}-x_{i}^{3}
for each
i\neq j+1
, the path
x_{j+1}-x_{j+1}^{1}-u-w-x_{j+1}^{2}-x_{j+1}^{3}
, connecting
v
to
x_{j},u,x_{j}^{1},x_{j}^{2}
, and adding the edge
x^{1}_{j}x^{1}_{j+1}
.
This completes the construction of
\mathcal{P}
.
We also define
\overline{k}
as a vectors that has 4 on every coordinate except for the
(j+1)
-th one, which is equal to 6. In particular, all values of
\overline{k}
are even, as required.

Let us verify that
(\mathcal{P},\overline{k})
is indeed a

-free permutation gadget for
\sigma
.

Claim 4.14.1
.

(\mathcal{P},\overline{k})
is a permutation gadget for
\sigma
.

Proof of Claim 4.14.1:

Notice that
P
is obtained from a linear forest
L
by applying
Lemma

4.13
with:

\displaystyle a=x_{j}

\displaystyle b_{1}=b_{2}=x^{1}_{j}

\displaystyle d=x_{j+1}

\displaystyle b^{\prime}_{2}=v

\displaystyle c=x^{2}_{j}

\displaystyle e_{1}=e_{2}=x^{1}_{j+1}

\displaystyle g=w

\displaystyle f_{1}=f_{2}=u.

Conversely, the desired linear forest
L
can be obtained from
P
, up to isomorphism, by the removal of the vertex
v
and edge
x_{j}^{1}x^{1}_{j+1}
.
It is straightforward to verify that all of its components are paths with endpoints
x_{i},x_{\sigma(i)}
for
i\in[\ell]
.
Moreover, each such path has 4 vertices, except for the one for
i=j+1
, which has 6 vertices.
(See also
Fig.

14
).

\lrcorner

Claim 4.14.2
.

\mathcal{P}
is

-free.

Proof of Claim 4.14.2:

Suppose, to the contrary, that some vertices
a,b,c,d,e
, in that order, induce

.
Consider the edge
bd
. It cannot be an edge between two canonical segments, as such edges are all maximal, contradicting the existence of edge
ae
. The only remaining edges are
uv,ux_{j+1}^{1},vx_{j}^{1},x_{j}^{1}x_{j+1}^{1}
, and
wx_{j+1}^{2}
, and one can readily check that none of them participate in any induced

.

\lrcorner

This completes the proof of the lemma for

Proof of
Lemma

4.11
for elementary swaps.

Case:
H=

.

Let
\ell\geq 2
and let
j\in[\ell-1]
and
\sigma
be an elementary swap that exchanges elements
j
with
j+1
.

We will construct a

-free permutation gadget
(\mathcal{P},\overline{k})
,
where
\mathcal{P}=(P,(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}),(y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{\ell}))
is a train.
Again, the vertex set of
P
consists of the following four
canonical
segments of vertices, in the described order:

V_{0}

consisting of vertices
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}
(which are input of
\mathcal{P}
),

V_{1}

consisting of vertices
u_{1},u_{2},u_{3},u_{4}
,

V_{2}

consisting of vertices
x_{\ell}^{2},x_{\ell-1}^{2},\ldots,x_{j+2}^{2},x_{j}^{2},x_{j+1}^{2},x_{j-1}^{2},\ldots,x_{1}^{2}
,

V_{3}

consisting of vertices
x_{1}^{3},x_{2}^{3},\ldots,x_{j-1}^{3},x_{j+1}^{3},x_{j}^{3},x_{j+2}^{3},\ldots,x_{\ell}^{3}
(which are the output of
\mathcal{P}
, we identify them with corresponding vertices
y_{i}
).

The vertices are connected via paths
x_{i}-x_{i}^{2}-x_{i}^{3}
for
i\neq j,j+1
, path
x_{j}-u_{1}-u_{3}-x_{j}^{2}-x_{j}^{3}
, and
x_{j+1}-u_{2}-u_{4}-x_{j+1}^{2}-x_{j+1}^{3}
.
This completes the construction of
\mathcal{P}
.
We set
\overline{k}
to the vector that has 3 on every coordinate except for the
j
-th and
(j+1)
-th one, which are equal to 5. In particular, all values of
\overline{k}
are odd, as required.

Let us verify that
(\mathcal{P},\overline{k})
is indeed a

-free permutation gadget for
\sigma
.
It is clear that it is a permutation gadget, as it is already a linear forest of the required form. So, let us we focus on verifying that it is

-free. We will actually prove a stronger statement, namely, that
\mathcal{P}
does not contains even

as a subgraph.

Claim 4.14.3
.

\mathcal{P}
is

-subgraph-free.

Proof of Claim 4.14.3:
Suppose that the gadget contains

as a subgraph, and let
a,b,c,d,e,f
be its consecutive vertices.
Consider the edge
cd
and note that it is under a pair of crossing edges
ab
and
ef
.
However, any edge containedin
V_{0}\cup V_{1}\cup V_{2}
can only be under some edges between
V_{0}
and
V_{2}
, but such edges are pairwise non-crossing.
Similarly, an edge between
V_{2}
and
V_{3}
can only be under some other edges between
V_{2}
and
V_{3}
, which are likewise pairwise non-crossing.
As these are all edges of
\mathcal{P}
, we conclude that the edge
cd
cannot exist, contradicting the existence of

as a subgraph.

\lrcorner

This completes the proof of the lemma for

4.5
Summary: excluding a single induced subgraph

Our next goal is to summarize the hardness results achieved throughout the chapter.

Theorem 4.15
.

MIS
is
NP
-hard in
H
-free ordered graphs if
H
has at least three edges or if it contains

,

,

,

,

, or the mirror image of any of these as an induced subgraph.

Proof.

Observe that if
H
contains

,

,

,

,

or the mirror image of any of these, the conclusion follows, respectively, from
Theorem

4.12
,
Theorem

4.1
and
Theorem

4.2
for the last three.
What remains is to prove
MIS
is
NP
-hard in
H
-free ordered graphs whenever
H
contains at least three edges.

Let us fix any ordered graph
H
and suppose that
MIS
is not
NP
-hard in the class of
H
-free ordered graphs.
We will prove that
H
contains at most two edges, thus proving the theorem.

First recall from
Section

1
that if the underlying graph of
H
is not in the family
\mathcal{S}
(forests of subdivided claws), then
MIS
is
NP
-hard already in the unordered case and thus also in the ordered case
[
3
]
.
Thus, we might assume otherwise. In particular,
H
is a subcubic forest.

Claim 4.15.1
.

H
is a linear forest, i.e., every component of
H
is a path.

Proof of Claim 4.15.1:

Suppose
H
has a vertex
v
of degree 3.
Since
H
is a forest, the neighbors of
v
form an independent set, and thus
N[v]
induces either

, or

, or the mirror image of one of these.
Then
H
contains as a subgraph either

or

(or the mirror image thereof), which, by
Theorem

4.2
, contradicts the choice of
H
.

\lrcorner

Claim 4.15.2
.

Every component of
H
has at most 3 vertices.

Proof of Claim 4.15.2:

Suppose otherwise. By
4.15.1
, this means that
H
contains an induced path with 4 vertices.
Such a path can be ordered in 12 ways (up to isomorphism), and disregarding mirror images, there are 8 possible orderings to consider:

,

,

,

,

,

,

, and

.
Observe that
H

•

4.4
,

•

4.2
,

•

nor

by
Theorem

4.2
, as they both contain

as a subgraph,

•

nor

by
Theorem

4.2
, as they both contain

as a subgraph.

Since we excluded all possible orderings of a
4
-vertex path, this completes the proof of the claim.

\lrcorner

Claim 4.15.3
.

The underlying graph of
H
does not contain an induced three-edge matching.

Proof of Claim 4.15.3:

We eliminate all possibilities. The three-edge matching can be ordered in 15 ways (up to isomorphism), and disregarding mirror images, only 11 remain. Observe that

cannot contain:

•

,

,

.

It is easy (yet a bit tedious) to verify that the list above is complete.
Thus, this completes the proof of the claim.

\lrcorner

Claim 4.15.4
.

The underlying graph of
H
does not contain an induced
P_{3}+K_{2}
, i. e., the graph consisting of two components, one being the three-vertex path
P_{3}
and the other a
K_{2}
.

Proof of Claim 4.15.4:

Suppose that
H
contains a copy of
P_{3}+K_{2}
as an induced subgraph, and let
u,v,w,x,y
be its vertices such that there are edges
uv,vw,xy
.
We consider the cases depending on the ordering of
u,v,w
: (i)
u\prec v\prec w
and (ii)
u\prec w\prec v
– all the remaining cases are symmetric.
First suppose that
u\prec v\prec w
.
If any of
x,y
is between
u
and
v
or between
v
and
w
, then
H
contains an induced copy of

or its mirror image, which is a contradiction by
Theorem

4.2
.
Therefore, the set
\{u,v,w,x,y\}
induces either

, or

, or its mirror image, which is again a contradiction by
Theorem

4.2
.
So now suppose that
u\prec w\prec v
.
If any of
x,y
is between
u
and
w
, then
H
contains an induced

, which is a contradiction by
Theorem

4.1
.
Furthermore, if any of
x,y
is between
w
and
v
, then
H
contains an induced

, which is a contradiction by
Theorem

4.2
.
Therefore, the set
\{u,v,w,x,y\}
induces either

or

or

, which contradicts, respectively,
Theorem

4.3
,
Theorem

4.5
, and
Theorem

4.2
as

contains

as a subgraph.

\lrcorner

So now suppose that
H
has at least three edges.
Recall that by
4.15.1
and
4.15.2
,
H
is a linear forest, whose every component has at most
3
vertices.
By
4.15.3
, there are at most two components that are not isolated vertices.
So it is only possible when one component induces a
P_{3}
, and the other induces a
K_{2}
.
However, this contradicts
4.15.4
, which completes the proof.
∎

5
Conclusion

Let us conclude the paper by pointing out some directions for future research.
Note that the hardness part of
Theorem

1.1
is close to a dichotomy into cases solvable in quasipolynomial time and those that are
NP
-hard.
Indeed, the (quasi)polynomial-time algorithms given by
Theorem

1.1
(1) and (2) are a strong indication that the corresponding cases of
MWIS
in
H
-free ordered graphs are not
NP
-hard, since otherwise every problem in
NP
would admit a (quasi)polynomial-time algorithm.
By contrast, the subexponential-time algorithm for

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\}
) introduce a super-linear blow-up in the size of the constructed instance.
It would be interesting either to replace them with tighter reductions or to design subexponential-time algorithms for those cases.
We conjecture that the former is possible.

Finally, we believe that the ordered setting is promising also for problems beyond independent set and coloring.
To the best of our knowledge, apart from the current paper and recent work on (
List
)
k
-
Coloring

[
41
,
28
]
such algorithmic questions in hereditary classes of ordered graphs have not been considered so far.
In particular, it would be interesting to investigate
Feedback Vertex Set
and
Odd Cycle Transversal
in hereditary classes of ordered graphs.
Both problems can be seen as generalizations of
MWIS
.

Feedback Vertex Set
can be equivalently stated as finding a maximum size (or weight) induced forest, i.e., an induced subgraph of treewidth at most 1 (while an independent set is an induced subgraph of treewidth 0).

Odd Cycle Transversal
is equivalent to finding a maximum size (or weight) induced bipartite subgraph, i.e., an induced subgraph of chromatic number at most 2 (while an independent set is an induced subgraph of chromatic number 1).
Both problems are among the best studied in hereditary classes of unordered graphs
[
1
,
23
,
14
,
40
,
17
,
2
,
13
,
20
]
, so they seem like natural next targets in the ordered setting.

We are grateful to the organizers and other participants for the inspiring atmosphere and fruitful discussions.
